A file that could not hold a manifest, such as a README or a lockfile, is still passed over silently. A candidate is compared against the files actually opened by (st_dev, st_ino) rather than by name, because a ConfigMap volume mount exposes each key twice -- as a symlink and as the real file under ..data/ -- and reporting the second took the Tekton Task from passed to evidence-incomplete. Which extensions the walk should read remains open and is a product decision; this change is the part that holds under every answer to it.

  • The admission webhook admitted what it could not evaluate, and said nothing (G337). Admitting is the right policy -- a fail-closed webhook that denies what it cannot read freezes the cluster, including the deploy that would fix it -- but the response carried no warning, so at the one point where a verdict gates a real action, a privileged container in a kind no rule reads went in clean and the cluster admin saw a blank response. _findings iterated report.findings and nothing else. It now also reads report.undetermined() and appends one warnings entry naming what was withheld: non-blocking, already the channel used here for cross-object rules that could not be evaluated, and already printed by kubectl. It is said even when there are findings, because "this object has problems" and "part of it was never read" are independent. A clean object still gets nothing.

  • Containers in a field not called containers were never looked at (G333). unread_pod_specs finds the shape of a pod spec in a kind no reader knows and reports the object as not audited, which withholds the verdict rather than inventing a finding. Its detector required a containers list, so three shapes that genuinely run containers read as clean: Tekton's spec.steps[] and spec.sidecars[], Argo's spec.templates[].container (one mapping rather than a list), and any pod spec carrying only initContainers. A privileged docker:24-dind step in a Tekton Task scored 100.0, exited 0, gated passed and verified allow. The discriminator has not moved -- a mapping is container-shaped only if it declares a string image -- so widening which key may hold one does not widen what gets accused: a CRD that declares replicas and endpoints, and a Kyverno policy that names Pod as a match target, are still not accused of hiding pods. A kind the registries do read is unaffected.
  • kube-noob protocol and the run_protocol tool said ready for a workload the audit had not read (G339). The status came from an absence of HIGH findings alone, so a privileged container that is blocked in a Deployment was ready in an Argo Rollout or any other kind no rule reads -- exit 0, remainingBlockers: [], and kubectl apply -f manifest.yaml printed under it -- while the audit summary inside the same result said undetermined. This is the tool the server tells a model to use first for a review. The status now follows the audit's own predicate: undetermined, with statusReason naming what was not read, and exit 5 from the command. blocked is unchanged and carries the reason as well when both hold. Scenarios the red team could not decide are listed under undecidedScenarios rather than only inside steps. A status the command's exit table does not know now exits 4 instead of 0. A workload the audit read in full is still ready, exit 0.
  • A product doc edit had broken the website's build, and only the website could tell (G336). The site lifts the ## Status section of docs/roadmap.md into /insights/ verbatim, and refuses three plugin-index strings on every page it writes, because that pull request is not open and the command does not work. A row naming the command was moved into ## Status here, so the site's own suite failed -- found a week later in a session sourced at the other repository. The row now says what is waiting without naming the command, and a test here holds those strings out of that section using the site's own extraction expression, so the check sits where the content is authored rather than where it is published. Verified by running the site's full suite against a patched product checkout: 34 passed, where it had been 1 failed, 33 passed.
  • GitLab's Code Quality report was empty for an undetermined gate (G334). An empty list is how that widget spells "no issues", so a gate that reached no verdict told the reviewer the change was clean. G328 fixed this for audit --format sarif and G331 for sarif_of_gate; codequality is the third renderer of the same report. It now carries the gate's undetermined line, one issue naming every rule that reached no verdict, and one per file not read or not audited. All at severity: info and never a blocker: the exit code is what stops a pipeline, and a coverage gap raised as a defect gets the whole report dismissed as noise. A Helm template or a file that is simply not a manifest is still not raised -- forbidsVerdict is the one marker, and reading it wrong would put an issue on every chart in the repository.
  • Three MCP tools answered as though they had read an object the audit refuses (G330). A privileged Deployment under apps.k8s.io/v1 is an apiVersion the API server would never serve and the audit refuses outright. posture_scan ranked it as a real apps/v1 Deployment anyway and reported workloads: 1, no attack paths and an empty notRead, so a model answering from that result told the user the workload was clean; the scan knew two families of wrong apiVersion -- routing kinds under another group, RBAC kinds under a legacy one -- and a workload kind fell past both. threat_model is built on the same inventory and said the same by saying nothing. Inventory.add now asks the audit's own refusal before ranking any kind it models, so there is one detector rather than a third local idea of what a wrong group looks like, and the refusal is recorded under the gated refused cause. An object with no apiVersion at all is still ranked, deliberately: the in-cluster backend's list items carry none. Separately, fedramp_ksi set verdict: undetermined after the indicator crosswalk, which in insertion order put it at byte 54,945 of 55,269 -- an MCP client that truncates a long tool result dropped exactly the word withholding the page's claim, and the model read a full KSI page with no caveat. The verdict is now first, so a 1 KiB prefix still carries it.
  • The SARIF the CI Action uploads did not say what the run had not evaluated (G331). G328 fixed that for audit --format sarif and left sarif_of_gate alone -- and sarif_of_gate is the renderer behind gate --sarif, which is the file scripts/ci/gate.sh hands to GitHub code scanning. So the uploaded artifact carried fourteen findings and nothing else for a report whose own controlSummary said fifty-two rules reached no verdict, and for an input made only of kinds no rule reads it carried zero results, which is how SARIF spells "nothing found". It now builds the same coverage record, with the two keys that differ from the audit's report normalised (unreadable rows carry file here and path there; the input uri comes from files, because a gate finding has no file). A file the walk declined to read as a manifest is the same absence and now gets the same result instead of being the one file-level gap with no alert. G272's kube-noob/undetermined result is unchanged -- the two answer different questions, and both stay.

  • verify answered allow for manifests the gate called incomplete, and two more readers of the gate's report did the same (G329). kube-noob verify -f on a privileged Deployment written under apps.k8s.io/v1 printed allow and exited 0, directly above the audit's own sentence that the object "was not audited, so this verdict is undetermined"; gate -f on the same file said incomplete and exited 1. The same held for a kind in the wrong case, for a ClusterRoleBinding to cluster-admin under a group the rules refuse, and for a control-plane rule that declined -- every reason the audit learned to call undetermined after verify was written, because verify kept its own list of them instead of reading the gate's. It matters most where nobody is watching: verify is the check a coding agent passes before kubectl apply, and the MCP tool handed a model "verdict": "allow". It is not-determined now (exit 5; a deny stays a deny). Two neighbours had the same shape. insight returned ran: audit and no findings for that object, with no word that the audit had reached no verdict; it now says so under skipped. The CI wrapper behind the GitHub Action, the Tekton Task and the GitLab template wrote evidence=complete for an undetermined gate whenever a Containerfile, a scanner report and a SARIF report were all supplied, because it read notRun and never the report's own incomplete; evidence is incomplete there now and because starts with the audit's reason.

  • The audit's SARIF said what it had not evaluated only where no consumer reads it (G328). audit --format sarif on one privileged Deployment emitted twelve results, every one a finding, and put all thirty-six statements about what it had not evaluated -- fifty-five rules that reached no verdict, by reason -- into invocations[0].toolExecutionNotifications. GitHub code scanning raises alerts from runs[].results; DefectDojo and the scanner operators import findings from runs[].results. So the file arrived as twelve findings and nothing else, and the undetermined half of the answer was destroyed at the first hop. The unevaluated rules are now one kind: review result naming every reason and every rule id, and each file that could not be read is one result anchored to that file; the notifications stay, so a reader of the invocation loses nothing. One result rather than thirty-three because thirty-three near-identical alerts are what gets a rule muted, and a muted rule is a worse outcome than the notification this replaced. Both new rules say how to close the gap and say that it is not a pass. The remedy already existed in the same file for the gate (G272) and for objects no rule read; the unevaluated rules were the neighbour nobody carried it to.

  • kube-noob mcp --tools incident|review|compliance|learn offers one job's tools instead of all fifty-six (G327). Fifty-six tools is more than most MCP clients list usefully and more than a model picks well between: the tools for the job in hand compete with fifty that do not. A profile offers fifteen to twenty-three. What it must never do is make a withheld tool look like a tool kube-noob does not have -- a model that cannot see kubenoob_fedramp_ksi and is not told why answers the compliance question from its own knowledge, and the user reads an unaudited answer in a session that looked audited. So the server's instructions name every tool the profile withheld, with the flag that offers it, and say not to answer from knowledge and not to claim kube-noob cannot do it. A test holds that every tool the hand-written instructions recommend by name is either registered or listed as withheld, never recommended and silently missing. An unknown profile name exits 2 instead of quietly offering all or nothing, and a profile changes only which tools are listed -- never what one answers, so two servers started differently give the same call the same bytes.

  • A ruff release could turn every open pull request red with no change to this repository. pyproject.toml asked for ruff>=0.8 in both the dev extra and the lint tooling, so the lint job installed whatever was newest at the moment it ran. A ruff release that adds a lint rule then fails a tree that passed an hour earlier, on every branch at once, and the failure names a style rule rather than the change under review -- which is the most expensive kind of red build, because nothing in the diff explains it. It also meant a contributor and CI were running different linters: ruff check passed locally on 0.16.9 and failed in CI on 0.17.6 with PLC0207 and an import-order rule that 0.16.9 does not carry. Pinned to ruff==0.17.6, the version CI was already resolving to, so the linter changes when someone decides it changes. mypy>=1.13 and pytest>=8.3 have the same shape and are not touched here: this is the one that has actually gone off.

  • Merging G272 into G281: one verdict predicate, one record of what was not evaluated, and five rules that now declare their subjects. Both branches had independently invented Report.undetermined() and not_audited, and the merge kept G272's predicate as the only one: a rule that declined on an object withholds the score, not just an object the reader refused. Where the two genuinely disagreed, the side that refuses to call something clean won -- so a decline now withholds the verdict, and KN-V002 (not KN-V001) owns a non-string apiVersion, because one field judged by two rules was two verdicts for one field. A platform or version guard, a partial bundle and an unconfigured policy are NOT unread objects: they are named in notEvaluated with their reason and keep the score, which is what G272 means by a quiet decline. Three integration defects that neither branch could see alone are fixed with it: a rule whose kind is simply absent declined with no reason at all and so was in NEITHER evaluatedRules nor notEvaluated, leaving controlSummary, the KSI page, the OSCAL document and the gate each with a rule they could not account for; partlyEvaluatedRules was inferred from a rule having findings and not having run, which G281 makes impossible by recording such a rule as having run, so that list would have been empty for every input on both the control summary and the KSI page, and it is now read from the audit's own record; and an exception that accepted an object no longer leaves the two control-plane rules that declined because of that same object holding the verdict open. KN-S037..KN-S041 declare their subjects like every other rule, and APIService and OAuth are in SUBJECT_GROUPS so they can. notEvaluated keeps the rule's own words rather than a count of them, the notAudited row keeps the apiGroup and line an exception matches on and the SARIF locates by, and verdictReason is the one name for the withheld-verdict text (alert_report already used it; scoreWithheld said it a second way). No exit code changes. Those five rules move to kube_noob/audit/cprules.py, unchanged, because the merged rules.py put the generated plugin copy 30 KiB over the directory's 256 KiB per-file limit; the walk that checks every rule declares the kinds it reads now covers both modules.

  • A rule about a kind that was not in the input counted as evaluated and clean (G281). The coverage record -- controlSummary in audit, audit_manifests and the gate, coverage in a posture snapshot, the KSI crosswalk's columns -- recorded a rule as evaluated when its check ran, whatever it ran over. kube-noob audit -f examples/kube-noob-exceptions.yaml therefore recorded 87 of 93 rules as evaluated on a file holding one ExceptionList (the other six had declined on the partial bundle): no Pod was "no privileged container", no Role was "no wildcard grant", no Service was "no NodePort", and the OSCAL document built from a Deployment-only directory reported 15 controls satisfied and excluded none. Five rules gated on the platform did the same: an OpenShift-only rule read as checked-and-clean on every Kubernetes bundle. This is the absence-read-as-pass the project exists to refuse, in the record that is supposed to be the refusal.

Every rule now declares what it is about (subjects= on @rule: a kind, alternatives such as Role or ClusterRole, the pod-spec kinds, or every object for the per-document validations), and a rule whose subject is not in the input does not run: it is notEvaluated with the reason no Service in the input, whatever else it could have read. A kind counts only in the API group the rules know it in, so an ACM or Knative Subscription, a Knative Service or somebody else's MachineConfigPool is not the subject (no operators.coreos.com Subscription in the input), and by_kind and workloads apply the same rule. A rule that needs two subjects (a Service and a pod spec; a NetworkPolicy and a pod spec; a PodDisruptionBudget and a replicated workload) needs both; a rule that reads a kind beside its subject names it when absent under rulesEvaluatedWithout (KN-S014 without ServiceAccount), and KN-N001 still fires on a whole namespace whose workloads no NetworkPolicy selects. Every rule that declines carries its own reason (part of a namespace was passed, so the namespace's NetworkPolicies were not passed; does not apply on kubernetes: ...), and a rule that declined on one object and reported on another is evaluated, with the decline kept as a note rather than listed as both fired and not evaluated. Subject labels are sorted, so the record is the same bytes under any PYTHONHASHSEED.

The reasons are in every embedding: controlSummary.rulesNotEvaluated and per-control notEvaluatedRules in the audit (JSON, YAML, and grouped by reason in the text view, with the count the JSON has), the MCP audit_manifests and fedramp_ksi results, audit --format sarif (one toolExecutionNotification per reason, beside the assumptions G269 put there), the gate's JSON, Markdown summary and HTML page (one coverage line: 45 rules evaluated, 48 not evaluated: no Service in the input (6); ...), the OSCAL selection remarks, a posture snapshot's coverage.notEvaluated (with the resource named when the cluster refused it), the posture report's controlStanding in JSON, Markdown and HTML, and the KSI page, which labelled every such rule "Declined to decide" and now says "Not evaluated" with the reasons. An OSCAL deliverable now lists under exclude-controls every control whose rules only read kinds the input lacks -- 10 of 31 on a directory holding one Deployment -- and reports fewer controls satisfied (7, where 0.9.2 reported 16). A snapshot from 0.9.2 carries no reasons and keeps its older shape; a diff between one and a new snapshot reports nothing new, fixed or regressed. The exit code is unchanged for an absent kind: it is not a finding and not unreadable evidence.

A third review found the group match drawn too tight, in three ways, each a kind of absence-read-as-pass in the other direction (a real object refused, so the rules about it did not run). An object with NO apiVersion was refused as "a kind of another group": the in-cluster backend's list items carry none, so a live posture snapshot or scheduled drift job evaluated 3 rules on a cluster where 0.9.2 fired 19 (KN-S001, S015, N007 among them). No group is an unknown, not a contradiction, as podspec.refused_group already held; and every live collector -- the posture snapshot's, the attack-path scan's and the operator inventory's, through one helper -- now records the apiVersion the object was listed under, so the stored object says which API it came from, as kubectl's output does, and KN-V001/V002 no longer report a live object as missing it. (The one visible consequence, for users of the in-cluster backend only: a folder of posture snapshots taken before this release has no apiVersion on its objects, so the first posture diff after upgrading reports that one field changed on each of them, and those two rules fixed; nothing else, and the scan and the inventory keep no history to diff.) Kubernetes' own retired groups -- extensions/v1beta1 Deployment, DaemonSet, ReplicaSet, Ingress and NetworkPolicy, apps/v1beta1, apps/v1beta2 -- were refused the same way: audit -f <legacy dir> --version 1.15 --fail-on high exited 0 where 0.9.2 exited 1, tests/fixtures/legacy-bundle.yaml lost KN-C001/C002/C009/C010/C014/V010 and gained a false KN-V011 ("selects no pod template"), because the Deployment was dropped while the Service beside it was read. SUBJECT_GROUPS now holds every group a kind has ever been served under, derived from the removed-API history the version rules read (REMOVED_APIS), never hand-listed; reasons still name the group the kind is served in today. OpenShift 3.x's ungrouped v1 is OpenShift's own history the same way: oc get -o yaml on 3.x wrote apiVersion: v1 for DeploymentConfig, Route, BuildConfig, Build, ImageStream, ImageStreamTag, Template, Project, SecurityContextConstraints, ClusterResourceQuota and the authorization kinds, so those are read as the kinds they are (KN-V002 still says the API is gone), from LEGACY_OPENSHIFT_V1, kind to the group it moved to, cited to openshift/api release-3.11 <group>/v1/legacy.go; v1 is accepted for exactly those kinds, so a core v1 Deployment is still not a Deployment. The neighbour of that shape -- a Deployment in example.com/v1 beside a real one and a Service -- is refused rightly and now SAID: the report's assumptions name the object as not audited, and KN-V011 and KN-N012 decline for that namespace instead of concluding "selects nothing" from pods they did not read. Four declarations were wider or narrower than their rule: KN-V010 judges only Deployment, StatefulSet, DaemonSet and ReplicaSet and KN-N001 fires only for long-running workloads and Pods, so each was "evaluated and clean" on a CronJob-only or Pod-only input and now names those kinds instead; KN-ST001 reads StorageClass and declares it as context; and the pod-spec kinds are spelled out inside an alternative label too ("no networking.k8s.io Ingress or pod spec in the input (no Pod, Deployment, ...)"). A test now walks every rule body for by_kind(...) and fails when the kind is declared as neither subject nor context. And the text view's one line a reader scans, NIST SP 800-53 controls with findings: CM-7 (5), ... AU-2 (0), listed every touched control with its count, so a control whose only rule was not evaluated read as checked-and-zero while the JSON beside it said notEvaluated: 1: that line now names only controls with findings, and a second line, controls not evaluated, in whole or in part: AU-2 (KN-S035), CM-7 (KN-N009, ...; 8 clean), ..., names every control with a rule not evaluated, with the rules and its clean count when it is partly both; the JSON's set and the text's are the same set.

A fourth review found that a refused object made the GATE pass. A Deployment under apiVersion: v1/, APPS/v1, apps/v1 or example.com/v1 was named in audit's assumptions and nowhere else: gate exited 0, its Markdown and HTML said "gate passed", its SARIF had no word of it, and the coverage line in all four formats said "no pod spec in the input" of a file holding a Deployment with a privileged container -- a typo in apiVersion turned a failing gate into a passing one. Now a refused object is never a pass: the report carries notAudited (object, kind, and a fixed reason: apiVersion is not a string, apiVersion is not well-formed (<group>/<version> or <version>), apiVersion names a group kube-noob does not know Deployment under), the text view prints it, the gate's JSON carries the list and its failures name it, the Markdown and HTML say "Not passed: N object(s) not audited" and never "passed", the SARIF carries the list as an invocation property and one note-level result per object, the posture snapshot's coverage carries it, and the verdict is undetermined: audit and gate exit 1, never 0, whatever --fail-on says. The subject reason for a refused kind is 1 Deployment not audited (...), never "no pod spec in the input"; a pod spec in a kind with no reader (an Argo Rollout) is 1 object not audited (pods are in a kind kube-noob has no reader for). This also means a bundle holding a same-named kind of another API -- a Knative Service, an ACM Subscription, a Volcano Job -- now exits 1 as undetermined where it exited 0 before, because the rules know that kind name and did not read the object; the note names it. apiVersion: null is a missing apiVersion (read, with KN-V001), not a refusal, and a non-string one is refused as "not a string" -- Python's None and 7 no longer reach a note or KN-V002's message; a slash-less or malformed apiVersion on a core kind (7, /v1 on a Pod) is refused the same way instead of being read as core v1. No input text reaches a reason: the reason a declining rule gives for a refused or unread workload is built from the kind the rules know and fixed phrases (a bidi override in a group name reached rulesNotEvaluated[].why in JSON and text), and the cleaned, quoted value appears in the assumption line only. KN-V011 and KN-N012 decline for a namespace holding pods in a kind kube-noob has no reader for (an Argo Rollout, a Knative Service) exactly as for a refused Deployment, through one helper. OpenShift 3.x bindings are read as binding whom they bind: userNames/groupNames (the deprecated fields openshift/api authorization/v1 types.go keeps "when supporting legacy clients and servers") and the SystemUser/SystemGroup subject kinds go through the one conversion the attack-path scan already had (kube_noob.audit.rbac_legacy, moved there so there is one), so KN-S016 names who gets cluster-admin instead of "[]" and KN-S028 reports system:authenticated bound through groupNames. The declaration walk fails loudly on a traversal it cannot classify (a ctx.docs comprehension, a filter, a .data.get("kind"), a helper from another module given the context) instead of returning an empty set, for every rule that does not declare "any object". The OpenShift refusal note says "the legacy ungrouped API (v1)", not "core"; a live snapshot's reasons say "read from the cluster" and "never requested from a cluster (not on the read-only allowlist)", never "in the input"; KN-V014 tells duplicates apart by API group as well as kind, namespace and name. The apiVersion stamp wins over an empty, null or non-string value in every live collector. One intended fixture drift: tests/fixtures/bad-bundle.yaml holds an apps.openshift.io/v1 Route, which 0.9.2 read as a Route and reported under KN-N002; it is now refused and named, KN-V002 still fires on it, and the fixture's verdict is undetermined. apps/ (a group with an empty version) is refused as not well-formed where 0.9.2 read it as apps and reported KN-V002.

A fifth review found the nearest neighbour still passing: a pod spec in a kind with no reader (an Argo Rollout with a privileged container), or a kind typo on a real one (deployment, "Deployment ", 7 under apps/v1), was exit 0 and score 100.0 while the same JSON said "1 object not audited". The three kinds of not-read object now come from ONE helper (AuditContext.not_audited): a refused apiVersion, a kind that is not a kind name (kind is not well-formed (an UpperCamelCase name), kind is not a string), and a pod spec in a kind with no reader (pods are in a kind kube-noob has no reader for); the engine's notAudited, every subject reason and the verdict are built from it, so a reason that says "not audited" is never beside an empty list. An unknown kind in an unknown group (example.com/v1 Widget) is neither: nothing claims to read it, it is not listed, and the verdict stays determined. The verdict word is now on the record, from one predicate (Report.undetermined()): summary.score is null and summary.verdict is undetermined (with verdictReason), the text headline says score=not determined (1 object(s) not audited), the gate's Markdown headline is ## kube-noob gate incomplete and its HTML masthead "The gate is incomplete", fix.after carries the same summary, ksi exits 1 and the fedramp_ksi result carries verdict and notAudited, and posture diff/report read coverage.notAudited into the report (JSON, Markdown and HTML) and exit 1. An accept path exists: an ExceptionList entry notAudited: {namespace, kind, name, apiGroup} with owner, reason and expiry (never a rule: entry) accepts one object; the row stays listed with acceptedBy, the verdict is determined again, and the SARIF help text points at it. The notAudited[].object and the note are cleaned once, where the row is built (sanitize.one_line, 120 characters), and resources and the "No bundled schema" note the same way: a newline in a name forged a second note row in the text view and reached the gate JSON and SARIF raw. KN-V014 keys duplicates on the kind's identity: one object across every group the kind was served under (extensions/v1beta1 and apps/v1 Deployment web; the two RoleBinding groups OpenShift serves as proxies), the group as well otherwise. userNames/groupNames entries that are not strings are refused by shape (userNames[1] is not a string), never printed as a Python repr; userNames: [] beside subjects says why nobody is bound (the list is set, so subjects are ignored, per openshift/api authorization/v1); posture scan reads OpenShift 3.x v1 RBAC through the same conversion as the audit. A non-string apiVersion ([], {}, 0, true, 1.5) gets exactly one message, "apiVersion is not a string" -- KN-V001's "missing" is for absent, null and "" only. The KN-NOT-AUDITED SARIF result is kind: review with the object's file and line and a partial fingerprint, so code scanning lists and tracks it.

Measured with the command named, kube-noob audit -f examples/<file> (one file, so the partial-bundle guards also apply; the count is the rules: line of the text view), rules evaluated before and after: exercise-answer.yaml and insecure-deployment.yaml 87 to 49; netpol-shop.yaml 87 to 46; netpol-admin-tiers.yaml 86 to 46; kube-noob-exceptions.yaml 87 to 9, with all 84 others naming the kind they waited for. (blueprint.yaml and problems.yaml are not manifests and the command refuses them.) G210's boundary "a bundle with no admin policy is evaluated and clean" is reversed: nothing to decide is not a failure to decide, and it is not a pass either. - Thirty-six wall-clock assertions stood in thirty-four tests, and the tight ones measured the CI runner rather than the code (G326). G263 made this exact fix for two of them and left the rest; two of the rest have since blocked merges. test_hostile_and_oversized_input_is_bounded_and_fast asserted time.perf_counter() - start < 1.0 beside an exact len(ceph.json_checks(many)) == ceph.MAX_CHECKS, and measured 1.0239 s and 1.0607 s on PR #77's test (3.11) job after the identical sha had gone 19 of 19 green on a workflow_dispatch run of it. The work it timed takes 0.001 s, so the budget had a thousandfold headroom and lost anyway: it measured a second of descheduling. test_a_post_that_cannot_get_a_slot_is_refused_with_503_within_the_wait asserted waited < 3 with the server's own SLOT_WAIT_SECONDS monkeypatched to 0.3 s -- ten times the deadline it was about, so it could not tell an honoured deadline from an ignored one -- and failed at 3.1 s.

The fix is one helper rather than thirty-six adjusted numbers. In tests/bounds.py a call site states the SHAPE of the regression it guards against -- linear work, cubic risk, catastrophic backtracking, a small multiple, fixed work, no hang -- together with the honest time it measured, and the shape names the smallest regression it must catch as a multiple of that. The ceiling is the geometric mean of the two, so the assertion sits as far above the noise as it does below the regression: a linear scan turning quadratic is about n times the work and these inputs are in the tens of thousands, so a tenfold ceiling catches it while a 1.05-fold ceiling catches only noise. The environment enters twice, because the two things that go wrong on a shared runner differ in kind -- multiplicatively for a coverage tracer, measured here at 1.0x on the regex-dominated ceph scan and 1.51x on the Python-object-heavy SARIF merge, and additively for a stall, which no multiplier models and which is what the ceph failure was. Both terms are frozen at import from the environment, not calibrated per session, so no fixture and no test order can change a later ceiling. Every failure now prints the elapsed time, the ceiling, how the ceiling was derived and the regression the assertion is for, because assert 1.06 < 1.0 is why these were dismissed as flakes rather than read. A ceiling that could not catch the regression its shape names raises VacuousBound instead of passing -- which is what it did for the ceph site, whose honest cost of one millisecond leaves no room above a stall and below a 25-fold cap removal, so that clock is deleted and the exact cap assertion it sat beside carries the contract alone.

Three sites the inventory missed were found by the guard rather than by grep, and two of them were the tightest in the suite: test_no_crafted_line_costs_more_than_a_few_milliseconds and test_a_long_non_matching_line_is_cheap asserted a per-call average of 15 ms against a measured honest 1.2 to 4.2 ms -- under four times -- and dividing by the loop count divides out the loop but not a stall. They time two hundred calls now, which is enough honest work for a ceiling above a stall and still below the 92 ms-per-call regression G252 fixed. The third was test_subscriptions_times_install_plans_in_one_namespace_is_linear, whose counted builds == 1 is the exact assertion and whose clock is the broader guard beside it. The two call sites that assert a deadline the code itself configures derive their ceilings from that constant instead of from a number of their own, so an ignored deadline fails where the old < 3 and < 5 could not -- and the server is built outside that block, because a ceiling derived from a deadline measures the deadline only if the block holds nothing else, and under seven parallel pytest processes building one took up to 4.5 s. Three contrast tests, whose whole signal is the gap between a fast call answered and a fast call queued behind a slow one, cannot be made loose without being erased: two now assert the ORDER in which calls completed, which no stall can move, and the third states the gap it needs and is refused outright when the slow side is too small for it, so its blocking side was widened from 1.5 s to 6.0 s rather than its ceiling raised.

Measured rather than assumed: every converted test was run twenty-five times with five other pytest processes competing for two cores, and the spread was read per site rather than taken on trust. That found one site a ceiling cannot help. test_diagnose_lists_its_signals_and_stays_fast_on_hostile_text honestly costs 5.0 s, so the linear shape asks for a 104 s ceiling and MAX_CEILING_SECONDS caps it at the 30 s the test already had -- under six times the honest cost -- and it measured 32.5 s, 35.7 s and 37.1 s in three of twenty-five runs. Raising the cap was rejected, because it would loosen the 30 s G263 chose for the memory demo and the 20 s for the cubic attack-path scan to buy headroom a single-process CI job does not need. So that site became a ratio instead, which is G263's own fix: it diagnoses half and full of each paste back to back and holds the ratio under 3.0, measured at 1.40 to 1.73 across twelve runs where quadratic is about 4.0, and both halves meet the same stall. The cap still sets the ceiling for the 20,000-event must-gather and the megabyte sanitize pastes, whose worst measurements under that load were 23.9 s and 18.4 s against it, and a failure there now says the ceiling was capped rather than reporting a derivation that did not happen.

What none of this catches, and is not asked to: a wrong answer produced quickly, and -- the one that matters -- work that got faster by being skipped, which is the shape of a false pass and which no timer anywhere can see. So every call site keeps the deterministic assertions it had, and several now carry only those. tests/test_timing_assertions.py parses the test tree with ast and refuses a new raw wall-clock comparison below the helper's own loosest ceiling, so the next tight budget fails on its author rather than on somebody's merge three weeks later. There is no allowlist of the existing sites, which would be a second source of truth edited by exactly the change the guard exists to stop; the exemptions are structural instead -- time.process_time, which does not advance while the process is descheduled, a ratio between two measurements taken on the same machine, and a stated ceiling at or above the floor, such as the documented north-star minute for the worst-case report. - A manifest whose apiVersion key was spelled apiversion passed the gate, and verify allowed it (G325). gate -f DIR reads every *.yaml under the directory and classifies the ones that are not manifests so a GitHub Actions workflow beside your charts does not fail the job. The membership test was exact-case, so a pod with apiversion: v1 and a correct kind: Pod got the workflow's answer -- "not a Kubernetes manifest (no document with apiVersion and kind)" -- and a privileged pod with hostNetwork, privileged: true and runAsUser: 0 was listed under skipped while passed stayed true, incomplete stayed false and the command exited 0; verify -f DIR, the check meant to run before the apply, answered allow. Kubernetes keys are case-sensitive, so the API server would have rejected that document as well: the file was never read as a manifest, and nothing said so in the verdict. A required key (apiVersion, kind) that is absent exactly and present as a spelling that differs only by letter case, surrounding whitespace or -/_ separators is now named as what it is -- ``a Kubernetes manifest whose key apiversion is spelled differently from apiVersion``` -- and that entry inskippedcarriesforbidsVerdict: true. From that one marker: a line infailures, sopassedis false;incomplete: true, so the Markdown headline reads "incomplete" rather than "failed"; an error-level notification and thekube-noob/undeterminedresult in the SARIF; and exit **5**, beside the oneunreadablealready uses and for the reason G126 gives -- a file the walk declined to read is not a policy violation, which a pipeline reads 1 as. There is no flag that waves it through:--skip-unreadableanswers a different question, and the fix for a key spelled wrong is to spell it right.verify -fappends the same reason toundetermined, so the verdict isnot-determinedand the exit code 5, through the mechanism the forty-odd command shapes verify cannot model already use;--allow-undeterminedstill turns that into 0. The document is NOT normalised and audited: auditing input the API server would reject as though it were valid would be the same false answer pointing the other way. The detection is deliberately narrow -- a kustomization, a Helm values file or a CRD fragment that carries akindand noapiVersionat all is still skipped in silence, because widening it that far would turn every repository scanned withgate -f .red -- and the negative half oftests/test_near_miss_manifest.pyis what holds that. A YAML file nested past the loader's depth limit, which_classifyalso returns a reason for, forbids a verdict now too: it was not read either. A structural test fails when a new skip reason reaches_classifywithout a decision beside it ingate.SKIP_REASONS, so the next reason added cannot silently inherit "this is fine, pass the gate". - **A compliance report can be all green because the checks that would have failed were removed, and nothing said so (G323).** The OpenShift Compliance Operator is where "compliant" is easiest to misread: aComplianceSuitecan reportresult: COMPLIANTwhile aTailoredProfiledisabled the rules that would have failed, while aComplianceCheckResultsaysNOT-APPLICABLE, while aComplianceScannever leftRUNNING, or while a rule the profile claimed to run produced no result at all. Four different absences, and every one of them looks like a pass to a reader counting statuses.kube-noob compliance resultsreads the operator's own objects -- ComplianceCheckResults, ComplianceScans, ComplianceSuites, Rules, Profiles and TailoredProfiles, from manifests (-f) or a must-gather -- and answers with the rules the profile **said** would be evaluated, eachpass,failornot-evaluatedwith the object that proves it: the tailored profile and the rationale it gave, the status the check actually carried, the scan that did not finish, or the silence of no result at all. A suite reportingCOMPLIANTover any of them comes outundetermined, never compliant, with its own claim printed beside the reason it does not hold, and the pass rate is null with that reason beside it rather than computed over rules nothing evaluated -- the shape the audit report already uses for its score. AFAILis never softened: an incomplete scan does not withdraw a failure the operator did report. Where aRulecarriescontrol.compliance.openshift.io/NIST-800-53`, those ids join kube-noob's own control map, so each control is evidenced by the operator, by a kube-noob rule, by both, or by neither -- a control the operator names only on a rule it did not evaluate and that kube-noob cannot check -- and none is ever reported as satisfied. There is deliberately no live read: the feature adds no allowlist entry, no RBAC and no credential, and an input with no Compliance Operator objects is undetermined, never "the operator is not installed", because a file set cannot prove that.

  • A status: that YAML gave as a list crashed the reader instead of being reported as unrecognised (G324). Every value the Compliance Operator writes -- check status, scan phase, scan result, severity, check type -- was compared with value in <frozenset>, and an unhashable value raises TypeError, which would have left exit 1 (findings) for input the tool simply does not recognise. One guarded helper now answers "not one of them" for anything that is not a string, and that answer is not-evaluated with the field named: the same for a lower-case pass, a Profile whose rules is a scalar rather than an empty profile, a disableRules that is not a list, a List wrapper whose items is a mapping, and a rule name carrying a soft hyphen, which is flattened and marked rather than joined to the rule it resembles. Found by the tests for this feature before it was pushed.

  • The directory skills told Claude to compare version numbers, and the comparison breaks on the next release (G321). All seven skills in plugin/kube-noob-skills read "if the version it printed is lower than 0.9.3 ... do not read its output as an answer. Say exactly this: this kube-noob is older than these skills need, so nothing has been audited." That is a number comparison done by a model, and 0.10.0 sorts BELOW 0.9.3 as text while being newer by PEP 440, so the next release would make every skill reject a perfectly good engine and tell the user nothing was audited. The capability check that already sat beside it -- a result's first key being forModel -- observes the engine instead of parsing a number, so it is now the whole gate, and the skills say explicitly not to compare versions. pipx install 'kube-noob>=0.9.3' is unchanged: pip resolves that correctly.

  • One builder change made two images unreadable to their own users, and fixing the first did not look for the second (G322). Containerfile.seed copied the sealed snapshot with COPY --chown=0:0 --chmod=0444 ... /seed/${SEED}. A COPY whose destination directory does not exist makes the builder create it, and a newer BuildKit applies --chmod to that directory too; 0444 has no execute bit, so the init container (1001) could not enter /seed and died with cp: cannot stat '/seed/jira-snapshot.tgz.enc': Permission denied. That is the same defect as G319's in Containerfile.db, three days later, and it is what kept e2e-kind (OpenShift 4.22, Kubernetes 1.35) red on main and on every branch. The snippet in docs/use-cases.md that tells a reader to build their own seed image carried it as well, so anyone following the documentation built the same broken image. Both now create the directory and make it traversable in their own step first, and tests/test_image_copy_modes.py refuses any COPY --chmod whose mode has no execute bit and whose destination directory is not created beforehand -- the class, rather than the two instances of it.

  • An init container that crash-looped could not be diagnosed from CI at all (G320). The e2e jobs' install handler printed kubectl get events and nothing else, so a failing init container produced "Back-off restarting failed container" and never what the container itself printed. The e2e-kind (OpenShift 4.22) leg failed this way on several branches and on main, and the one thing that would have explained it was never captured. Both handlers now call scripts/ci_pod_failure.sh, which prints the events, the pods, and every container of every pod -- init containers first, and --previous as well as the current attempt, because a crash-looping container's current attempt is usually the empty one.

  • The public data image became unreadable to its own user, and no commit did it (G319). Containerfile.db copied the archive with COPY --chown=0:0 --chmod=0444 ... /kube-noob-db/. A multi-source COPY into a trailing-slash destination makes the builder create that directory, and a newer BuildKit applies --chmod to the directory it creates as well as to the files. 0444 has no execute bit, so the directory could not be entered: the image's own user and the chart's init container, both 1001, got cp: cannot stat '/kube-noob-db/kube-noob-db.tgz': Permission denied, while root still passed through on DAC_OVERRIDE. The same job failed when re-run against a commit that had passed it eighteen hours earlier, which is how the cause was placed in the runner rather than in the tree. The directory is now created and made traversable in its own layer before the files land, and CI reads the archive as 1001 right after the build, so the next occurrence names the directory instead of surfacing as a confusing cp error.

  • Nothing checked a change at the moment an agent was about to apply it, and an agent left to choose would not have asked (G280). kube-noob could audit files, gate a pipeline and warn at admission, but the step in between -- a coding agent typing kubectl apply -- had no check of its own, and four reviews agreed that an agent will not reliably call one; the harness has to. kube-noob verify gives one verdict for a change: allow, deny or not-determined, never an allow for what it could not read. The change is manifests (-f) or a command line (--command), read as data by a new bounded shell reader that sees through sh -c, bash -lc, eval of a literal, env, sudo, timeout, xargs, find -exec, watch, backslash-newlines, quotes inside a word, a full path, the k alias, here-documents, cat ... | and kustomize build ... |, and a script file in the working tree -- and calls everything else (a command word built at run time, eval "$(...)", code piped into a shell, a kube tool named inside python -c or ssh, an alias for kubectl) not determined. Files are resolved inside the working directory, with no link followed and no network; helm install|upgrade of a local chart is rendered the way gate --chart renders it; a kustomization is read when it only lists resources; a --type merge or --type json patch is applied to the object a single-object -f names. A URL, -f - with nothing on stdin, a strategic-merge patch, exec, a delete other than pods by name, drain, --prune, --context or KUBECONFIG on the line, a flag or subcommand verify does not know, and a file the audit skipped (kubectl would still send it) are each not determined, with the reason. A deny lists the findings the change adds at or above --fail-on with file, line, rule, one-line fix and the admission column, and the kube-noob fix command when running the fixer shows it clears a failing rule; grants of cluster-admin, the privileged SCC or wildcard RBAC in a command deny through the memory verifier's own list. The manifests are judged by gate.run itself, so verify -f X allows exactly when gate -f X passes and denies whenever it fails on findings; verify is stricter in one place, a pod spec in a kind no rule reads, which the gate only notes. With --against, only findings the change adds count. Exit codes add no meaning: 0 allow, 1 deny, 5 not determined (--allow-undetermined makes it 0; a deny stays 1, since more evidence cannot turn it into an allow). The verify_change MCP tool takes the command and the text of the files it names and reads nothing from disk.

kube-noob hook claude-code is a Claude Code PreToolUse hook built from the current hooks reference: it passes a command that starts no cluster change untouched (about 0.2 s), runs verify on one that does (about 0.6 s), and blocks with exit 2 and the findings on stderr on a deny and on its own errors, and asks the user with the reasons on not-determined -- a crashed check is not a pass, and it answers before reading kube-noob's configuration, whose errors exit 4, which Claude Code would have let through. It never prints permissionDecision: allow, which would skip the user's own prompt. kube-noob hook install --claude-code [--user|--project|--local] prints the settings; --write merges them, keeping every other hook, atomically, refusing a link; the snippet sets onFailure: block. The plugin carries the same hook in hooks/hooks.json, off until its new verify_cluster_commands option is turned on, so an update starts blocking nobody. KUBE_NOOB_HOOK=off in Claude Code's own environment is the human's escape hatch; the same text inside a command does nothing. SECURITY.md says plainly that a client-side hook does not stop an agent with a shell that means to get past it; the admission webhook is the server-side control. The gate now also carries the audit's assumptions (a pod spec in a kind it cannot read, an assumed version) in its report and summary, which it had been dropping. kube-noob demo verify walks through a deny with its fix, the fixed file allowed, and a command that is not determined. The command reader extends the classifier diagnose uses: kubectl -n shop get pods (a global flag before the subcommand), --dry-run=client and the k/kubecolor names were all labelled as needing approval before; --dry-run=server stays a change, as the dry-run tier (D) treats it. Not built here: what the change newly exposes (reach) against --against, which needs the blast-radius code on another branch.

An adversarial review of the first version found commands it allowed that change the cluster in ways it had not read, and all are fixed. A file written earlier on the same line (cp evil.yaml app.yaml && kubectl apply -f app.yaml, sed -i, git checkout, a redirect, any program it does not know) now makes the verdict not determined. kubectl's optional-value flags (--validate, --dry-run, --cascade) take a value only with =, so in kubectl apply --validate false -f bad.yaml the false is a file kubectl sends and verify had not read; a value left over like that is not determined. kubectl patch -f no longer allows by subtracting the file's own findings. The dry-run reading was the first --dry-run, read past --, with none counted as a dry run; it is now the last before --, none and false are not dry runs, and diagnose shares the fix. Substitutions inside ${...}, $((...)) and unquoted here-documents are found; cd is followed before a script is read; KUBECTL_EXTERNAL_DIFF, --kuberc and KUBERC are not determined, and a global flag that writes a file (--profile-output) counts as a write for what follows; kubectl diff and cluster-info dump are labelled as running something, in diagnose too; kustomize build --enable-exec (and --enable-helm, --enable-alpha-plugins) runs code and is not determined. verify -f DIR now reads what kubectl reads -- dotfiles, .json, exact extensions, subdirectories only with -R -- where it had skipped .hidden.yaml and allowed. Cheap bypasses are closed: other deployers (helmfile, argocd, flux, skaffold, terraform, istioctl, velero, kubeadm, kapp) ask unless they only read, KubeCtl, kubectl.exe, kubectl1.30, helm3, $'kubectl', git -c alias..., man -P, awk 'BEGIN{system(...)}', vim -c and env -S are read or not determined, and the hook covers the PowerShell tool, asking when a command names a kube tool. The hook now asks the user (permissionDecision: ask, shown with the reasons; Claude Code denies it in a -p run) instead of blocking on not-determined, and reads [ ... ], [[ ... ]], source .venv/bin/activate and a Makefile or package.json recipe instead of refusing them -- on a reviewer's 161 ordinary commands it now asks about none, and on 40 realistic ones it allows 35 and asks about 5; it allows a day-2 change that cannot add a manifest finding (a restart, a scale, a label without a security key, deleting pods by name, a patch by name touching only safe fields), models a kustomization's namespace and images and helm's --set, and denies a literal credential in set env. It answers inside its own deadline -- the evaluation runs in a thread and a spent budget exits 2 -- where a slow input had taken 21 s; a total byte cap and a cap on helm's output bound the work. The plugin's hook had been registered as uv run with "onFailure": "block" on every Bash and PowerShell call of every plugin user: everyone paid uv's start-up on every command (about 1.0 s warm, 5.9 s with an empty cache), every command of a user who never turned the check on was blocked whenever uv could not start or resolve offline, and on Windows without Git Bash a hook that could not even parse would have blocked everything. It is now a POSIX sh guard that reads the opt-in from CLAUDE_PLUGIN_OPTION_VERIFY_CLUSTER_COMMANDS, the variable Claude Code exports to plugin hooks, and starts no program while it is off (about 2 ms); on, it runs uv run and blocks the command itself (exit 2, one line on stderr) when uv is missing or its run fails, on any Claude Code version, so the plugin no longer sets onFailure. hook install, which writes an entry only for a user who asked for it, keeps onFailure: block. The opt-in is read by one rule everywhere (exact, ASCII case ignored, nothing stripped). What stays open for a plugin user who turned the check on: a uv that stalls past the 60-second timeout, and Windows without Git Bash, where the check does not run; SECURITY.md says both. Repository text in a hook message is framed as data, and text shaped like an instruction is flagged. helm is run only from an absolute PATH entry outside the working tree -- in gate --chart too, where a ./bin/helm in the repository could have run -- and a chart with a link in it is refused. hook install --write reports a kube-noob entry with a different matcher, flags or onFailure instead of "unchanged", and refuses a read-only file and a file with repeated keys. With --against, the findings the change does not add are listed (up to 20) rather than dropped. AGENTS.md now says how verify's 0/1/5 and the hook's 0/2 fit the exit-code table, and SECURITY.md names what the hook does not stop.

A second review found four more ways a change was allowed and a way the hook failed open, all fixed. The shell reader treated any program it did not know as harmless, so aws-vault exec prod -- kubectl delete ns prod, npx kubectl ..., strace kubectl ... and forty-odd other wrappers were allowed, as were commands fed on standard input (echo 'kubectl delete ns prod' | at now, | ssh bastion, at now < cmds.txt, make -f -). The default is now opaque: a program the reader does not model is checked for a kube command in its arguments -- by name in any case or version, followed by one of that tool's subcommands that is not read-only -- and for text naming a kube tool on its standard input, and either makes the line not determined; a program that names none (uv run pytest, pytest -k helm, brew install helm) still passes. make read the wrong Makefile under --file=, -fFILE and -C DIR, ignored include, $(shell ...), inline (target: ; cmd) and backslash-continued recipes and command-line variables, and treated --eval as a target; all are read now. npm, pnpm, yarn and bun scripts are read in the package --prefix, -C, --dir, --cwd or -w names, and npm exec, npm x, pnpm dlx, yarn dlx and bunx are opaque programs. sudo -nu admin, --user=admin and timeout 30 -- cmd lost the command they wrap; function f { ...; } hid its body. Day-2 commands that are escalations now ask: a cloud-identity annotation on a service account, expose --external-ip (CVE-2020-8554), oc expose svc (a Route), --from-file or --from-env-file outside the tree, helm plugin install; a URL with a password in set env is denied like a password. set env of a variable that loads code (LD_PRELOAD, NODE_OPTIONS, JAVA_TOOL_OPTIONS ...) asks, and so do fc -s and zsh's r, which re-run a command from history. kubectx, kubens and config set-context --current --namespace are local and pass. The hook's deadline was a thread join, which a regular expression running in C does not yield to: a Makefile line of 250,000 spaces took the rule pattern 62 s and the plugin hook was killed at 60 s, which lets the call through. The check now runs in a child process (fork, or a fresh interpreter where there is none) that is killed at the budget; the Makefile rule pattern is replaced by a linear reader and lines over 4,096 characters are not read; the lexer's patterns match at a position instead of on a copy of the rest of the line, and the brace-list one no longer backtracks; the plugin guard kills a uv run at 45 s and blocks. An answer that cannot be written to stdout blocks. Every input-derived token in a hook message is quoted between « » with one fixed sentence saying it is not an instruction, and text shaped like one is flagged. --against /dev/null is an empty baseline, --cni is checked (gate and verify, exit 2), --help lists exit codes 0, 1, 2, 4 and 5, hook install writes the absolute path of the kube-noob that ran it, and the helm lookup excludes the project's tree (its git root) rather than whatever directory the command runs in, so a working directory of $HOME no longer excluded every helm. A Makefile that includes a file missing from the tree that no rule builds now passes (make stops); it was not determined.

In the text view a finding's path and file name, an exception's owner and reason, and every line of the detection-coverage block, are cleaned: a ConfigMap key with a newline and an escape sequence forged a whole [CRITICAL] line, and it carried a raw ESC into the gate's GitHub annotation too. The gate's Markdown keeps assumptions and declined rules under separate headings; an exception whose rule declined is reported in exceptions.unusedNotEvaluated and no longer told to "delete it if the object is gone"; and the audit_manifests description and the plugins' reading guides tell an agent to read declined and notEvaluatedRules.

A third review found three more launch shapes that were silently "not a server", so beside one clean API server or etcd the rule read clean -- the same class three times, so the fix is structural (tests/test_control_plane_review3.py). Recognition is now fail-safe: a container is not the API server or etcd only when nothing points at it -- no image whose name has the binary as a dash-separated component (etcd-amd64, hardened-etcd, kube-apiserver-amd64, hyperkube; not etcd-operator), none of its flags, no ETCD_* variable, no mention of it or of k3s, rke2 or k0s as a command word anywhere in argv, args, env values or a script -- or when it is positively something else: etcdctl, k3s agent, or a script on the etcd image made only of etcdctl and coreutils. A path counts as the program only in a bin or sbin directory, . or /, and never as a --flag= value, so tar czf b /var/lib/etcd and velero backup --path=/var/lib/etcd are not etcd. command: [] is the image's entrypoint, as no command is. A binary path at the end of a script or before ;, && or a newline is found. k3s, rke2 and k0s are found after their global flags and through env, busybox, tini and scripts, and an AlwaysAllow passed through --kube-apiserver-arg is read as the CRITICAL finding it is. A rule that found something on one object and declined on another is named in the new controlSummary.partlyEvaluatedRules (and per KSI indicator, per OSCAL control remark, and as a "partly evaluated:" line in the text view, SARIF and the gate), with its findings still counted as findings; declined[rule].decided says how many objects it read in full. Also: ABAC or Webhook in --authorization-mode declines the authorizer (the policy file or webhook is not in the input) and still reads anonymous authentication; an empty --config-file= falls through to ETCD_CONFIG_FILE, as etcd does; the insecure port (--insecure-port/--port, --insecure-bind-address/--address, each pair one variable) is a CRITICAL finding only on a stated 1.19 or older, with an effective port above 0 (8080 by default) on an address that is not loopback -- 1.20 to 1.23 refuse to start with a non-zero port (checkNonZeroInsecurePort) and 1.24 removed the flags, so those, an assumed version and a value pflag refuses (08080) decline; etcd 3.6.4's booleans and the ignored legacy flags are read, and --enable-v2, which 3.6 refuses, declines unless the image says 3.5; a kubeadm ClusterConfiguration and an OpenShift KubeAPIServer are named in declined as seen and not read; and the gate's "Assumptions" section is an allowlist of the assumed-version lines, everything else staying under "Not evaluated in full". A fourth review (tests/test_control_plane_review4.py) corrected the insecure-port releases above against the v1.19.0-v1.24.0 sources and found the rest of the class: the objects that WRITE these flags -- kubeadm ClusterConfiguration (map and v1beta4 list forms), Cluster API KubeadmControlPlane/KubeadmConfig and their templates, the kube-system/kubeadm-config ConfigMap, a kind cluster's kubeadmConfigPatches, a k0s ClusterConfig and a Rancher provisioning Cluster -- are now read for the settings that decide on their own (AlwaysAllow, an explicit anonymous-auth: true, a plaintext etcd URL on a network address are findings) and named as seen and not read for the rest; flag words inside a script or an env value, an absolute path to the binary in a script's command position from any directory, an env value that is that path, an envFrom with an ETCD_ prefix, a quoted name (e''tcd) and a Windows kube-apiserver.exe are evidence; hyperkube-amd64 and origin-hyperkube images are; find, tar and sort no longer count as safe in an etcd-image script; typed lists (PodList, DeploymentList) are opened like List, nested lists too, and an item with no kind takes the list's; core PodTemplate is audited; the gate's HTML carries the not-evaluated and partly-evaluated lines; a KSI row says how many partly evaluated rules it did not name. A fifth review caught a regression in that shared change and its cost: the fourth round left a whole list unopened when any item was not a mapping, so a privileged Pod beside a trailing null item went unaudited by every rule (exit 0 where main gave exit 1), and opening a list rescanned the line map once per item (a 9,000-pod PodList took minutes and returned 81,001 findings through the MCP server, past the cap every other command enforces). Every list whose items is a list is now opened, an item that is not a mapping is its own document that KN-V001 reports and the control-plane rules decline, the line map is split in one pass, and the 1,000-document cap applies to what comes out of a list for every command alike -- audit, gate, ksi, fix and the MCP audit_manifests all refuse a 1,500-item PodList with the same message. An empty typed list stays as the record that the kind was read and held nothing. Also: kubeadm's v1beta4 list keeps every repeated entry and kubeadm sorts them by (name, value) before pflag's last-wins (cmd/kubeadm/app/util/arguments.go v1.31.0), so the pairs are sorted the same way; a form that does not match its kubeadm apiVersion (a list under v1beta3, a mapping under v1beta4) declines, as kubeadm cannot decode it; a Rancher machineSelectorConfig with a machineLabelSelector declines naming the selector, and k3s/RKE2's key+=value is read as a second flag, so authorization-mode+=AlwaysAllow is the finding it is; a declined configuration object names the files, extraEnvs and extraVolumes it skipped; an insecure port outside 0-65535 or a zoned address (fe80::1%eth0, which Go's net.ParseIP refuses) declines; a flag name with a character neither parser's names have (--Anonymous-Auth=true) declines as a flag the server refuses rather than being read as a finding; and the KSI text and HTML print morePartlyEvaluated as they print moreNotEvaluated. The headline score is withheld while any rule declined on an object: summary.score is null with summary.scoreWithheld naming the rules (2 rules not evaluated in full: KN-S038, KN-S039), and the text view prints score=not determined (2 rules not evaluated in full), because score=100.0 printed above "KN-S038 was not evaluated in full" was a number beside an unread rule. The quiet declines of a partial bundle keep the number; the per-control counts are unchanged; Report.score() still returns the number, and only the eval grader may ask for it by name. None of the counts below moves with this round: no file in examples/ has a control-plane container, and their reports gain only an empty partlyEvaluatedRules.

A sixth review (tests/test_control_plane_review6.py, with the reviewer's inputs copied under tests/fixtures/control-plane/review6/) found the score still printed beside the three OTHER records that something was not read, and the neighbours of the fifth round's fixes. The headline score is now withheld whenever ANY record says something was not audited -- a rule that declined on an object, a pod spec in a kind with no reader, a container whose image an operator supplies, an object in a group the reader refuses -- through one predicate, Report.score_withheld(), that every renderer reads: text, JSON, YAML, HTML, Markdown, the gate, MCP, fix's after and the eval grader, whose minScore check now fails with the reason where it passed at "score 100" on a report audit printed as not determined. A PodList item whose kind is 7 or [List] (truthy, so never "missing", and unchecked once the schema lookup failed) scored 100 with exit 0 beside "containers were NOT audited": KN-V001 now reports a non-string kind or apiVersion, so it is exit 1; KN-S038 and KN-S039 decline on any document holding a pod spec no reader looks at (items under spec, kind: podlist, a sequence of Pods as one list item, which unread_pod_specs now walks and names by document and line); a typed-list item with kind: null or kind: "" takes the list's kind, as the API's decoder reads a zero value. A list wrapper with items twice lost the Pod under the first (PyYAML keeps the last) and was then refused as "no document has both apiVersion and kind" when both it and its Pods did: the loader now keeps every object under every items with the line map rebuilt, carries the wrapper's own duplicate-key records (items, kind, apiVersion) on the first opened document, and KN-V018 fires with the wrapper's line and says the API server refuses the whole list; a file whose documents all came out of a list is never refused as not a manifest, and an empty list of either spelling (kind: List or PodList with no items, which kubectl get -o yaml writes for an empty namespace) gets no KN-V001 and exits 2 with one message for both: "held no items, so there is nothing to audit", where the empty PodList was a HIGH finding and the empty List a false message. kubeadm's v1beta4 sort runs on the RAW name (ArgumentsToCommand, identical at v1.31.0 through v1.35.0, sorts anonymous-auth before anonymous_auth) and pflag folds _ after, so anonymous_auth: "false" beside anonymous-auth: "true" reads false, as the API server does; the v1beta3 map sorts the same way; an apiVersion other than v1beta3 or v1beta4 (v1alpha3, v1beta5) declines as one kube-noob does not read instead of being read by shape; a bare YAML boolean or number as a value declines, because kubeadm's strict decoder refuses it for a string field; kubeadm's own configuration kinds and kind's Cluster get no KN-V001 for a metadata.name they do not have. k3s and RKE2 --kube-apiserver-arg values are read by one function for both the argv and the Rancher config, exactly as k3s's GetArgs reads them: one leading -- trimmed (--kube-apiserver-arg=--authorization-mode=AlwaysAllow is the CRITICAL finding it is), key-=value (prepend) and key+=value (append) both a second flag, a bare key the flag set to true. pflag trims an IP value (= 0.0.0.0 serves on 0.0.0.0, and was declined with a false "pflag refuses it"); a flag name of a shape no server defines (--anonymous-auth-, --123) declines the container as one that does not start, where the defaults were reported (a misspelling inside that shape is still not caught: the full flag table is per release and is not carried). Cluster API KubeadmConfig.files and preKubeadmCommands/postKubeadmCommands on every kubeadm kind are named as scripts that may rewrite the manifest; a Rancher machineSelectorConfig given as a mapping is read as the one selector it is, and machineLabelSelector: {matchLabels: {}} selects every machine, as {} does. The coverage numbers above were recomputed and did not move.

A seventh review (tests/test_control_plane_review7.py, inputs under tests/fixtures/control-plane/review7/) found two wrong verdicts inside the sixth round's shapes and a decision the sixth round had left open. Undetermined is now exit 1 (the owner's decision, the same as G281's; the rule and why it is not a 5 are written down in the exit-code section of AGENTS.md, which this entry is the first use of): whenever anything was not read -- a rule declined on an object, a pod spec no reader looked at, an operator-supplied image, a refused group -- Report.undetermined() says why, summary.score is null with that reason as summary.scoreWithheld, audit exits 1 whatever --fail-on says, the gate is incomplete: true and passed: false under the headline "gate incomplete" (JSON, Markdown, HTML, and in the SARIF as an invocation property plus one note-level kube-noob/undetermined result), the MCP tools carry it, and the eval grader marks every finding-derived check (forbidRules, maxSeverity, schemaValid, minScore) not evaluated with the reason, where rule KN-S038 not triggered passed on a server that ran AlwaysAllow. The records are the five of docs/architecture.md: cannot_decide(rule, why, resource) with a fixed-phrase why, Report.not_audited (notAudited in the JSON: {object, kind, why}, replacing the sixth round's internal unread), Report.not_evaluated (notEvaluated: stage not requested, or declined on N objects), the one predicate, and every renderer printing it once. A kind: List whose only items were empty typed lists scored 100 with exit 0 and "gate passed" (zero objects audited as a perfect score): the all-empty check now runs before the opened-from-a-list exemption, an empty wrapper is never a resource, and items: null or a bare items: is the same empty list, so all of them exit 2 with "held no items". items twice where one occurrence was not a sequence lost every Pod under the sequence one, put KN-V001 on the wrapper and let fix write the loss back: the loader now joins the SEQUENCE occurrences and names each other one in KN-V018 ("items on line 15 (occurrence 2) is a mapping, not a list, and was not read"), no list wrapper of any shape gets a metadata.name finding, a nested wrapper's record names the wrapper that has the key ("in the List that holds this PodList"), and fix refuses -- exit 1, nothing written, nothing printed as output -- any input with a key twice, in an object or a wrapper, because the API server refuses that file and rewriting it last-wins (or writing the objects of one occurrence as a whole file) silently dropped a privileged Pod. fix --format json prints JSON. The k3s/RKE2 argv and the Rancher config now share the decision block as well as the parser (anonymous-auth=true on k3s server was nothing while the Rancher config said MEDIUM): both read --kube-apiserver-arg values in the order GetArgs emits them (keys sorted on the cleaned raw name, so anonymous_auth=true then anonymous-auth=false is true), decide AlwaysAllow, an empty authorization-mode= (AlwaysAllow by upstream's default), an explicit anonymous-auth and the insecure port exactly as the static-pod reader does, and decline a flag name no server defines (a padded entry is one: nothing is trimmed, as configfilearg trims nothing) or an authorization chain the API server refuses (a comma value is one argument, so AlwaysAllow,anonymous-auth=true names a mode it rejects); a value strconv.ParseBool refuses for --anonymous-auth declines the whole server in every reader, because pflag parses every flag before the authorizer runs. kubeadm from 1.36 keeps overrides in their written order and merges an entry with a mergeMethod into its own default (kubernetes master cmd/kubeadm/app/util/arguments.go, fetched 2026-11-13; through v1.35.0 every pair is sorted), so --version 1.36 reads the mirror of what 1.35 reads and a mergeMethod entry declines (before 1.36 as a field the strict decoder refuses); a v1beta4 entry with a non-string name, a field the Arg type does not have, or a key twice declines, a missing value is --name= and the flag's own parser decides, and a declined entry is said even beside a decided one. $(NAME) in argv is expanded as the kubelet expands it (third_party/forked/golang/expansion/expand.go v1.31.0): from a literal env[].value in the same container, $$ as one literal $, and anything else -- valueFrom, envFrom, a value that itself references a variable, a name the manifest does not define -- declines naming the variable, so --anonymous-auth=$(ANON) beside ANON: "true" is the finding it is and $$(MODE) is the literal mode kube-apiserver refuses. A Rancher selector that names the control-plane role (rke.cattle.io/control-plane-role, node-role.kubernetes.io/control-plane or master, as matchLabels or an Exists/In term) is read as reaching the API server; a selector is printed as one-line YAML through the output boundary, a scalar machineSelectorConfig entry is named, and an empty files: [] is nothing to read. A typed-list item of another kind (kind: Deployment in a PodList) inherits no apiVersion and is KN-V001 "missing apiVersion", not KN-V002 for a value the user never wrote. A null, empty or non-string kind is named <no kind> or <kind not a string> in every message, a null list item "(null)", never a Python repr. The HTML report labels only the assumed-version lines "Assumed", as the gate's Markdown does. operators inventory reads a wrapper's duplicate items through the same loader and names the file as one the API server refuses, so the answer is partial, not listed. The sixth round's weaker tests now assert the finding's text, line and object, and one table of --kube-apiserver-arg values holds the k3s argv and the Rancher reader to the same answer.

Coverage records move accordingly. On the bundled examples/insecure-deployment.yaml for Kubernetes, summing each control's rules per state, from origin/main (68fdc3a) to this change: through audit_manifests on the whole bundle (partial_bundle=false) controlSummary goes from 157 clean / 2 not evaluated to 155 / 18; through kube-noob audit -f (a partial bundle) from 151 / 11 to 149 / 27, with notEvaluatedRules naming 12 rules; and kube-noob audit -f --oscal from 15 controls satisfied, 15 not satisfied and none excluded to 13 satisfied, 15 not satisfied and AU-2, AU-12 and SC-28(1) excluded. In the KSI view (fedramp_ksi with partial_bundle=false, the whole bundle) KSI-CMT-LMC goes from 32 clean / 0 not evaluated to 31 / 3 (KN-S035, KN-S037, KN-S038), KSI-CMT-RMV from 41 / 1 to 41 / 3 (KN-S037 and KN-S038 beside KN-S036) and KSI-CNA-MAT from 11 / 0 to 11 / 3 (KN-S037, KN-S039, KN-S041); kube-noob ksi -f moves the same three indicators by the same rules. - A plugin installed from the Claude directory could explain kube-noob's rules but not run one audit (G276). The directory only takes Markdown, JSON and images from an organization that is not Verified, so kube-noob-skills had no engine, and its skills could only describe what the CLI would have said. Each of its seven skills now checks for the kube-noob CLI the user installed (kube-noob --version), runs it through Claude's Bash tool, and reads the exit code, the three-valued verdicts and what was not evaluated from the JSON.
