The plugin is still Markdown and JSON only: no server, script, hook, allowed-tools, !-command line or package launcher, and nothing that turns on cluster, web or model access.

An adversarial review of the first version found twenty defects, all fixed here. The ones that mattered most: - Pasted text could run as a shell command. It went into a file through a heredoc with a fixed delimiter, so a pasted line equal to the delimiter ended the heredoc and the next line ran. The skills now write pasted text with Claude's file-writing tool into a private per-session folder (umask 077, mktemp -d), check the write, and remove the folder afterwards. The fixed /tmp/kn-* paths, which two sessions shared and a planted link could redirect, are gone. - A crash read as a finding. An exception nothing caught left Python's exit status 1, which is this CLI's "findings at or above --fail-on". A Role with resources: 1 crashed audit that way, and so did wrong-typed fields in redteam, fix, vuln threat, protocol and posture scan, so a pipeline read a crash as a policy violation. A rule that meets a field of the wrong type is now recorded as not evaluated, named in assumptions, and kept out of the evaluated list. The readers outside the rules use a small typed accessor (kube_noob.shape) that raises one exception naming the field, so each catches exactly a wrong shape and nothing else. A red-team scenario that cannot read its input is undecided, never mitigated. A document fix cannot read is left exactly as it was and listed in manualFollowUps; that includes a securityContext that is not a mapping, which fix used to replace and record as a change from nothing. A workload the threat model, or a Service the attack-path scan, cannot read is reported under notRead. Reviewers' debug-mode fuzz runs found 12 such sites, then 14 more (a metadata, spec.selector or capabilities.add of the wrong type), then 5 more (PVC accessModes: .inf, a memory quantity of .nan, containerPort: [1], and kind: [Pod] in fix), and a rerun found one this branch had added (a whole document that is a set or a number, read as a mapping by the check for a non-string kind); all are fixed. Anything that still escapes main exits 4 with one error: internal error (...) line; KUBE_NOOB_DEBUG=1 shows the traceback, and makes main re-raise a bare KeyError or ValueError too, since the CLI cannot tell those from a refusal. A test sets every distinct field of the rule fixtures, kind and apiVersion included (633 fields), to an int, a string, null, a list, a mapping, a boolean, a float, NaN and infinity, runs every one of those 5,697 files through audit and each field once more, with one of the shapes, through one of redteam, validate, vuln threat, posture scan, fix and protocol in turn, all with KUBE_NOOB_DEBUG=1, so the catch-all cannot hide a crash. KUBE_NOOB_EXHAUSTIVE_MUTATION=1 runs all seven commands on every file (39,879 runs). - A second review found an absence still read as a pass. A PodDisruptionBudget with selector: 7 made KN-C009 decline, and RT-UPGRADE-BLOCK, which reads only KN-C009, then called the upgrade path mitigated. Each scenario now records the rules it reads; when one of them was not evaluated the scenario is undecided and names it, and redteam prints its assumptions in JSON and text. One Role with resources: 1 hid the wildcard Role beside it from KN-S015: a rule that fails is now run again without the objects it fails on alone, keeps its findings for the rest, and names what it left out. posture scan dropped a privileged Deployment whose metadata was 7; it now ranks it as Deployment/<metadata not readable>, and --fail-above exits 5 instead of 0 when an object or a read was refused (see the third review below); the length the 50-entry notRead list withholds is counted in notReadWithheld. A Secret is recognised before its shape is checked, so its name never appears whatever its spec is. A document whose kind is not a string is named as not audited instead of passing clean. The skills had been written for 0.9.2, which has none of this; they now need kube-noob 0.9.3 or later, install kube-noob>=0.9.3, and say an older engine is too old instead of reading it. Phrases the skills put on a command line are single-quoted, so $(...) and backticks in them run nothing. - A third review found the gate too strict and four more absences read as a pass. The exit 5 above fired on everything under notRead, which on a live cluster lists every CRD it does not serve (KServe, Ray, Routes on plain Kubernetes) and in a bundle every RoleBinding to a built-in role it does not carry, so a live gate could never pass and metrics-server's own manifests exited 5. posture scan now lists what it was refused under refused (an object of the wrong shape, a read the cluster forbade, timed out on or answered with something that is not JSON) and exits 5 on those only; what was absent stays in notRead and fails the gate only with the new --require-complete. system:auth-delegator and kube-system/extension-apiserver-authentication-reader, which every aggregated API server binds, are known beside cluster-admin, admin, edit and view. --help, use-cases and the openshift skill say what exit 5 means; the text view and the exit-5 line count what the 50-entry lists leave out. The rules a red-team scenario reads were recorded in one module-level list, and the MCP and REST servers run tools in threads: a clean run cleared what a bad one had recorded, and the bad PodDisruptionBudget came back mitigated three runs in four. The record is now a context variable. A PodDisruptionBudget whose selector, labels or budget had a type the API server refuses was read as "no PDB", or as a budget of nothing, and 36 of 45 such shapes left RT-UPGRADE-BLOCK mitigated; KN-C008 and KN-C009 now read it strictly and are undecided, naming it. KN-C008, KN-C009, KN-C012, KN-C013, KN-C014, KN-V011, KN-V012, KN-V023, KN-N012 and KN-ST002 judge one object by another (the PDB, HPA, Service, Secret, NetworkPolicy or PVC beside a workload): when any object of those kinds, or any workload, has a document, kind, metadata, metadata.name, metadata.namespace, metadata.labels or spec of the wrong type, or a workload's pod template, its labels or its containers, or an HPA's scaleTargetRef has one, the rule is undecided and names the object. Deeper fields of a partner are covered for PodDisruptionBudgets only; a pod template that is absent (template: null) is read as absent. KN-V001 now reports a metadata.name that is not a string. The "evaluated without" note was unbounded (154 KB on a thousand broken objects) and followed the input's order; it now names the first ten in sorted order and counts the rest. A document that is not a mapping, has no kind, or has a kind that is a known one spelt in another case or with spaces (pod, ' Pod') is named as not audited; an apiVersion that is not a string is said separately, since the rules read such an object by its kind. A Rollout, Argo Workflow, Kubeflow job, Notebook or SparkApplication whose spec is not a mapping is refused by posture scan instead of vanishing. A phrase starting with - reached the CLI's option parser (search '--help' printed usage, and the skill took that for an old engine): the skills now put a phrase last, after --, write a cause or fix as --cause='...', and forbid a leading -. - Merged with the blast radius (G273) and the absent-versus-unread work (G279), so the two kinds of not-read are one rule. Inventory.unread(entry, cause) is the one place that decides which list an entry goes to, and every site that records something as not read names its cause from a closed list (refused, absent, unmodelled, unplaced, ambiguous); a table-driven test drives every site and a source check fails on one that writes to a list directly. Refused, and so gated: a field of the wrong shape (a spec, metadata, subjects, rules, verbs, a List whose items is not a list, an exposure field that is not the shape the API requires), a legacy OpenShift binding the server would refuse, two different Roles under one bound name (neither is read), a live list the cluster forbade, timed out on, answered with something that is not a list of what was asked (G279's structural check) or not JSON. Not gated, listed, and still leaving their leg not determined: a role the input does not carry, an aggregated role nothing in the input feeds, a routing object or a CRD's Role this scan has no reader for, a Knative Route, a bound Role with no namespace, two different Services under one name, a front to a Service the files do not carry, and a live list the backend's own words say the cluster does not serve -- including a resource of an aggregated group (OpenShift's Routes on plain Kubernetes), which G279 still refuses to call absent for the verdict but which did answer, so the kind gate passes. posture reach carries the same refused list, with notReadWithheld and refusedWithheld, in its JSON and its text view, and its --require-complete now exits 5 on anything under notRead as well as on a leg not determined. Under KUBE_NOOB_DEBUG=1 a bare KeyError or ValueError is no longer re-raised out of main: it reaches G279's entry point, which prints the traceback through the output boundary and exits 4, so the fuzz tells a crash from a refusal by that traceback. - The CLI's JSON reached the model uncleaned, and its not-read keys were at the end. KUBE_NOOB_FOR_MODEL=1 now does for the CLI what tool_runner does for MCP results: every string goes through kube_noob.sanitize, the result says untrusted: true when something was neutralised, knowledgeAsOf is stamped, and the keys that say what was not read come first. Unset, the output is unchanged. The skills also write output to a file and print the exit code, because a result over a tool's output cap was cut before unreadable. - Ordinary checks wrote to the data folder. audit, diagnose, insight and policy explain added rule and playbook ids to learning.json. The skills now run with KUBE_NOOB_LEARNING_ENABLED=false. Learning progress is written only when the user reads a lesson, submits an exercise or answers a quiz. - "Exit 0: nothing found" was false for most commands. redteam, vuln report, posture scan, insight and others exit 0 with findings. Each skill now has a table giving each command's exit meaning, verdict fields and not-evaluated fields. A test runs every command on a fixture and fails when a field the row cites is missing, or when the output names a not-read key the row omits. The openshift skill no longer claims VEX and KEV are applied without --vex and --kev. - The fallbacks could download without consent. kubectl noob --version fetches kube-noob through uvx when kube-noob is missing, and the directory's checklist holds any pinned launcher for a reviewer. The skills now use only an installed kube-noob.

Smaller fixes: - helm-audit --format json printed [CHART] lines after the JSON. - fix -o and generate -o printed nothing on stdout under --format json; their report is now that JSON. - The no-krew install was four lines, so a FAILED digest still extracted the archive. Every kubenoob_* tool the full plugin's skills call is mapped to the CLI command its directory counterpart runs (33 of 37), or dropped with the reason stated in the test: the two live-cluster tools, the team-memory write, and the removed-API table, which has no CLI command of its own.

A remote MCP server pointing at the user's own kube-noob serve through userConfig would be admissible and is not shipped. Claude Code reports an unset URL option as Invalid MCP server config ... URL is unset or invalid, so every install that does not run serve would start in an error state (SECURITY.md).

On the krew side: - docs/install.md has a "which install, and what can run" table and a kubectl noob path that needs no krew. - The krew archive now carries LICENSE, which krew's pre-submit checklist requires. - The archive is gzip with stored blocks, so its digest is the same under zlib and zlib-ng. - The manifest's description no longer lists commands; one of the three it listed, policy explain --from, did not exist. - The script's Homebrew hint names the documented tap. - The manifest on main describes an archive the next release builds; krew/SUBMIT.md has the owner's steps for after that release.

  • Ten of the next ten merges were going to conflict in the same two files, and a conflict resolution that produces no code still gets a resolution wrong (G297). Every change here prepends a bullet to this file's Unreleased section and appends a G<n> row to docs/guidance.md, so two branches cut from one main insert at the same line in both files and every merge of main into an open branch stops there. With seven branches queued that is ten hand resolutions of two files nobody disagrees about, each one a chance to drop somebody's bullet or duplicate a row. Both files are append-only by construction, so .gitattributes now gives them merge=union: both sides' lines are kept and the merge does not stop. The list stops at those two. A document whose lines are rewritten -- docs/roadmap.md, docs/limits.md, docs/use-cases.md, docs/compliance.md, docs/safety-contract.md, all generated by scripts/doc_numbers.py or scripts/compliance_doc.py -- keeps the ordinary three-way merge, because union would keep both versions of a line both sides rewrote and leave two different module counts in one sentence; a test merges that case and asserts it still conflicts. The result is checked by what already checked it: scripts/doc_numbers.py --check, scripts/safety_contract.py --check and tests/test_guidance.py, which refuses a duplicate G<n> and a row whose cited test does not exist.
  • A published advisory against a pinned transitive dependency stops every branch's CI, so the pin moves the same day. pip-audit on requirements.lock reported CVE-2026-126360 against opentelemetry-api 1.45.1, which anthropic brings in; the fix is 1.45.2. The pin moves in requirements.lock and in the plugin's uv.lock, and the plugin copy is resynced. Nothing kube-noob does changes: the package is a transitive dependency of the Anthropic client and the audit is the gate that caught it.
  • The defect classes adversarial review keeps finding are now a document in the repository, and AGENTS.md sends every author to it before they push (G296). Eleven branches went through about thirty read-only review rounds this week; every one had passed the gate with tests, and about three hundred confirmed defects fell into twenty-six recurring classes -- a skipped input read as complete, a refusal read one layer up as an absence, the container's own metadata dropped while its contents were checked, a fix applied to one surface and not its siblings, a cap set above the resource it protects. The brief the reviewers worked from lived in a chat session and would have vanished with it. docs/review-classes.md keeps each class with the shape it took and the test shape that holds it; the numbers are stable (append, never renumber) and a test keeps the table gapless and the count in AGENTS.md true. Nothing in the code changes.
  • The first five minutes: four things a new user hit before any feature mattered (G277). A version outside the known-issue index read as "no known issue": known-issues 'etcd leader changed' --version 4.16.20 exited 0 with "matches": [] and a note naming the releases the index holds, and nothing a program reads said 4.16 was not one of them. Results now carry versionCoverage with three values -- determined (the release is indexed, so an empty list is an answer), not-determined (it is not, with the reason) and not-narrowed (no usable version) -- in the text view, JSON, YAML and the MCP result. diagnose, build diagnose, insight, the incident watch, the live diagnostic bundle, the must-gather report and case draft, and the gate carry the same statement as knownIssuesCoverage, and the gate lists the lookup under "Not run". A version the index cannot answer for does not change an exit code (known-issues has no --require-complete, and exit 5 means unreadable evidence, not a dated index, as for notCheckedBeyond); the exit codes that did change are listed at the end of this entry.

The README's headline commands named examples/... files that exist only in a checkout, so after brew install the first command a stranger copied answered No such file or directory. kube-noob examples copy DIR writes the examples the package already ships (never replacing a file without --force, never writing through a symbolic link, checking every target before writing any), examples list says what each one is for, and the README now starts with that step; a test runs every README command that names examples/ from a directory that is not the checkout. demo <name> --keep DIR keeps what a demo writes (demo must-gather printed "wrote report.md" into a directory it then deleted), so its printed commands rerun there.

diagnose had no text view: it printed about 216 lines of YAML whatever --format said, while demo diagnose showed the ten lines a person acts on. It now defaults to text like policy explain, known-issues and insight: the likely playbook, read-only steps first, then what needs approval labelled as such, denials, reported issues and every caveat the structured result carries. If a script reads kube-noob diagnose output as YAML without --format, add --format yaml; JSON and YAML under their flag are byte-for-byte what they were (every consumer in this repository already passes --format json). insight given text it does not recognise now lists what it recognises, from the router's own table, and a bundled sample that works, instead of only "nothing recognised". demo --list keeps its columns aligned past must-gather, and demo doctor is the new name of the demo that runs doctor (kube-noob posture is the attack-path scan); demo posture still runs it and says so, and its recording is now doctor.gif (demos/posture.tape is gone).

A review of the above found seventeen more ways to get the same wrong answer, all fixed. A pre-release, nightly or OKD version (4.16.0-0.nightly-..., 4.15.0-0.okd-...) was "not narrowed" and printed nothing, so a nightly must-gather printed the old "None of the findings matches a known issue"; versions are now read once, by their minor and z-stream, for the statement, the matching and the gate alike, and such builds are not-determined (OKD is never answered from OpenShift Container Platform release notes). A z-stream newer than the newest one the notes reach (4.22.40 against 4.22.18) is not-determined with indexedThrough. " 4.20.0" matched nothing while the statement said determined, and a 5,000-digit z-stream exited 4; gate --version is now checked. A fix in another release is no longer phrased as update advice for a version the index cannot answer for. selftest --cases scores such a case not-determined rather than passed. build diagnose, the incident headline and every other text view say it, and a test finds every consumer of the index in the code. examples copy turns a full disk, a dangling link or a control character into exit 4 or 2 with nothing left behind (it wrote a 0-byte file before), leaves identical files alone so running it twice works, and quotes the directory in the commands it suggests. The diagnose text view puts every value from the input on one line (sanitize.one_line, the G279 rule: a hidden character inside a name is shown as <U+200B>, never spliced out), names causes the input did not single out "possible" rather than "likely", counts the causes and evidence it does not show, prints the approach and any injection flags, keeps an Alertmanager payload's own version beside a --version that overrides it, and says when only the end of a long input was read.

A second review found what the first round left. The matcher read the HEAD of a long paste while diagnose and insight read its end, so an issue described at the end of 105,515 characters answered "no reported issue"; it now matches the last 100,000 characters, as they do, and says so (inputTruncated, coverage not-determined). Every tool model and selftest --cases refused the pre-release, nightly and OKD versions this entry teaches the matcher to read, with a raw validation dump: one version grammar (kube_noob.versions) now decides on every command and tool -- the grammar operators issues --openshift-version set (R5): x.y or x.y.z, ASCII digits without leading zeros, optionally a build after - or +, taken exactly as typed, so a leading v or white space around the value ("4.21\n" from an unstripped variable) is refused, not read as the version it resembles -- plus a release-image tag (4.21.3-x86_64 is the release 4.21.3). --version and --openshift-version share the parser and R5's one sentence (exit 2), and the tools' version and openshift_version fields refuse the same values with it; a padded value that reaches the index through a label narrows nothing and the coverage statement names the padding. 4.21 and 4.21.0 are the same release for versionConflict; 4.21 gets no z-stream update advice. examples copy rolls back on Ctrl-C too, works relative to the directory it opened (so one swapped for a link after the check is not followed), falls back to a rename where hard links are not supported, and refuses invisible characters in the directory name. The README's fallback for releases without examples copy is now a plain curl of the two files, which works in bash, zsh and fish whatever installed kube-noob; the earlier one needed an absolute shebang and failed under #!/usr/bin/env, pyenv shims, pip's /bin/sh launcher and fish. sanitize.one_line is sanitize.visible plus a whitespace collapse, with no rule of its own (G279's); the diagnose text view shows team memory's cause, commands (verbatim, needs-approval labelled), versions and risk acceptance; the gate with an OpenShift platform and no --version says reported issues were not looked up; a live debug whose version read failed says so instead of "No version was given"; insight carries an Alertmanager payload's version and any conflict, and lists what it recognises in JSON too; demo --list --format json lists posture under doctor (aliases) rather than as a demo of its own.

Exit codes that changed, measured against main: known-issues, diagnose, insight, build diagnose and ticket with --version 4.21.0-rc.1, 4.20.0-okd-scos.6 or 4.21.3-x86_64 exited 4 with a validation dump and now run (0; ticket 1 when the key is not in the local copy, as for any version). With text that is not a version (latest, 4, 4.20.x, v4.21.3, " 4.21") they exited 4 and now exit 2, the code for a bad flag value; gate did the same for latest and 4 (4 to 2) and accepted 4.20.x (1, findings) and now refuses it (2). selftest --cases --gate N can now fail (1) where it passed (0), because a case whose answer depends on reported issues for a version the index cannot answer for is scored not-determined, counted and not passed. Ctrl-C during examples copy exits 130 with one line and nothing left behind; a write failure there exits 4. - KN-S010 is critical for more host paths: audit and gate --fail-on critical exit 1 where they are mounted (G273). A hostPath volume was critical only for /, /etc, /var/lib/kubelet, /proc exactly and the docker, containerd, cri-o and cri-dockerd sockets. It is now also critical for /etc/kubernetes, /var/lib/kubelet/pki, /var/lib/kubelet/pods, /root and /var/lib/etcd and anything under each; for the podman and dockershim sockets wherever they are mounted from; for a runtime's socket directory (/run, /var/run, /run/containerd, /var/run/containerd, the cri-o, podman, docker and cri-dockerd ones); for any directory that holds one of those (/var, /var/lib); and for /proc/<pid>/root, read as the path under it (/proc/1/root is the host's /). //etc, /etc/ and /etc/../etc resolve to /etc first. This makes /run and /var/run critical, which they were not on main: they hold every runtime socket, so that is correct. Measured on 49 single-volume DaemonSets against main: audit --fail-on critical and gate --fail-on critical go from 0 to 1 for /etc/, //etc, /etc/../etc, /etc/kubernetes, /etc/kubernetes/pki, /var/lib/kubelet/pki, /var/lib/kubelet/pods, /var/lib/etcd, /root, /root/.ssh, /run/podman/podman.sock, /var/run/dockershim.sock, /run/containerd, /var/run, /var/lib, /var and /proc/1/root (17), stay 1 for /, /proc and the docker, containerd and cri-o sockets, and stay 0 for the other 27 -- among them kube-proxy's /run/xtables.lock, CNI state (/var/run/calico, /var/run/cilium, /run/flannel, /etc/cni/net.d), CA bundles, /etc/localtime, /etc/machine-id, /proc/sys/net, /var/run/secrets, /var/lib/containerd, /var/lib/docker, /var/log, /dev, /sys, /boot, /home, the kubelet's device-plugin and plugin-registry directories, /ETC, a relative etc and the Windows paths. Two directories under /etc/kubernetes are carved out, exactly and with what is under them, as /etc/cni/net.d is not /etc: /etc/kubernetes/node-feature-discovery (the node-feature-discovery worker chart and the OpenShift NFD operator) and /etc/kubernetes/secrets-store-csi-providers (the GCP secrets-store CSI provider), neither of which holds a credential. A readOnly mount is not downgraded: read-only /var still reads the kubelet's credentials and every pod's Secrets. On well-known charts and manifests (hostPath paths read from their templates): critical, and real, for the secrets-store CSI driver and the AWS provider (/var/lib/kubelet/pods: every pod's mounted Secrets), Velero's node-agent (the same), the EBS CSI node and Multus (/var/lib/kubelet, and Multus's / and /run), KubeArmor (runtime sockets and /run, /var), Calico, Cilium, Falco and Datadog (/proc, and Falco's /etc, Datadog's /) and the NVIDIA container toolkit (/); not critical for the NFD worker, the GCP, Azure and Vault CSI providers, node-problem-detector, or Multus thin. explain KN-S010 lists the rule. The attack-path scan labels the same paths, and only those, "node takeover".

  • Privacy: route and Ingress credentials are masked before they leave the cluster backend (G273). cluster_get (and every live reader) returned in clear: an OpenShift Route's header actions (spec.httpHeaders.actions.request|response[].action.set.value, an Authorization: Bearer set at the router) and an nginx Ingress annotation whose snippet sets a credential header (proxy_set_header Authorization ...) -- both on main too -- and, on this branch, Gateway API and Istio query-parameter values and a user:password@ in a redirect hostname. Every value under a route's header or query-parameter block, a Route's header actions, a snippet that sets an Authorization, Cookie, API-key or token header, and a userinfo in a route string are now masked; header and parameter names stay.

  • posture reach: what one workload reaches if it is compromised, as a JSON graph and a Mermaid diagram (G273, V3 done). posture scan answered "what does the internet reach?" and had no way to start from the other end: the pod a scanner just flagged, or the one an incident is about. Its paths stopped at the first capability and dropped an unexposed workload entirely, so a cluster-admin CronJob behind no Route had no blast radius at all. kube-noob posture reach deployment/web -n shop -f k8s/ (and the MCP tools posture_reach and, with cluster access, cluster_posture_reach) starts at one workload and returns five legs -- exposure, token, RBAC, Secrets, node -- each reached, none or not determined: the ServiceAccount whose token its pods hold (automounted or projected), each binding that names it, what that binding allows, the Secrets it may read by RBAC or reach by creating a pod (names and scopes; contents are never read), the node when the pod spec can escape to it, and which namespaces or the whole cluster that covers. The object named is answered from its own spec -- a Pod because it is what runs, a ReplicaSet or a Job because it can differ from its owner's template; its owner chain comes along, with what about it differs from its owner. Every edge carries the object and field that prove it and the evidence line posture scan prints for the same step, because it is built from the scan's own reading rather than a second one. --format json|yaml prints the graph (schema kube-noob/blast-radius v1, with a test holding every emitted document to it), and --format mermaid draws it, with the limitations and what was not determined as note nodes.

The half that matters is what the input cannot show, and a first version got it wrong in seventeen ways an adversarial review found before merge -- the worst of them a truncated graph that cut a privileged cluster-admin pod's capabilities and then summarised what was left as "reaches no capability". What holds now: the summary is computed before anything is cut, and truncation keeps the strongest capabilities, the node escape and the gaps first; a ServiceAccount, Role or aggregated ClusterRole that is not in the input, a stand-in for a built-in role, a duplicated name, a field that is not the shape the API requires, a bound rule this scan does not classify, a projected token for another audience and a live list that was refused each become a not determined leg with the reason, never a none; two objects with the anchor's name are an error, not a pick. A set of files cannot show the bindings or Services it leaves out, so for files none is said only of what the pod spec decides, and the answer is never complete unless --whole-bundle asserts the files are everything that applies; --require-complete exits 5 otherwise, and a reference that matches nothing exits 2. The graph is bounded in nodes, edges and bytes (96 KB for an MCP caller, diagram included), and says what it withheld. Every Mermaid label is escaped and every text field is one line, because an object name is input: a name carrying a quote, %% or a newline could otherwise add a click handler to the diagram or forge a row in the text view. --format mermaid on any other command exits 2.

posture scan changes too, because the blast radius reads the scan's own primitives and they were wrong in the same places. It now ranks the escalation rights the Kubernetes RBAC good-practices page names that it did not before -- TokenRequest (serviceaccounts/token create), creating a Secret (which mints a long-lived token the account can use if it can also read Secrets or create pods), exec and attach by get (WebSocket), ephemeral containers, PersistentVolume creation, admission-webhook control, and CSR creation with approval -- plus every capability the audit's KN-S005 calls dangerous, an unmasked /proc and Windows HostProcess. A projected serviceAccountToken volume is a token even with automount off. A RoleBinding in another namespace that names the account through a group or the system:serviceaccount: User is no longer skipped, and a path into that namespace names it rather than the account's own. admin, edit and view apply the aggregate-to-* labels of ClusterRoles in the bundle, aggregation resolves through chains whatever the label names, and an aggregated role keeps its own rules. Two ServiceAccounts of one name that disagree on automount take the worst case. Typed *List documents are read. The inventory is put in one canonical order, so the scan is byte-stable when the same documents arrive shuffled -- it was not, on main either. Measured on every fixture under tests/fixtures/posture and examples/: seeded.yaml gains one path (score 5: the built-in edit lets admin-sa create Secrets, which mints a token for any ServiceAccount of ops), its first five paths are byte-identical, and every other file still has none; two standing-limitation sentences are corrected on all of them.

A second adversarial review found that six of those fixes had moved the defect rather than removed it, and nine more; all are fixed with a test each, and the neighbour of each was tried. RBAC is read as the API server reads it: a resource matches as *, its exact name, or */<subresource> (Kubernetes ResourceMatches), so */exec and */proxy are no longer plain reads; each rule is classified resource by verb, so one that reads Secrets and patches nodes is ranked for the first and not determined for the second; and a right is harmless only if it is on a short named list, each entry with its source -- self and access reviews (information about authorization, not a right to act), token reviews, leases, events, status writes, role and binding writes without bind or escalate, OpenShift's scope impersonation (bound to every authenticated user, and "purely tightening" by its own definition) and a TokenRequest for the account itself. Creating a Secret mints a usable token only with a way to read it back or mount it, so secrets create alone is not determined rather than ranked (the built-in edit has both, so seeded.yaml's score-5 path stands). Measured on an app with a leader-election Role, an operator ClusterRole with system:auth-delegator, and the bootstrap bindings: 8 of 10 legs determined, from 6. What was answered none is now an unknown or an answer: a Gateway API HTTPRoute attached to a Gateway and an Istio VirtualService bound to a non-mesh gateway are exposures (and the read-only allowlist and the chart's read Role gain httproutes and virtualservices); a routing object of a kind kube-noob does not read (Contour, Traefik, Ambassador, the other Gateway API routes, a Knative Route) makes exposure not determined and is named; hostPort and hostNetwork are an exposure on the node's network; a Route, Ingress or HTTPRoute whose Service is not in the files is a gap, also under --whole-bundle, and the v1beta1 Ingress serviceName is read; a Secret volume holding a legacy ServiceAccount token (a Secret of type service-account-token, or any Secret at the token path) is a token -- only the Secret's type and annotation are kept, never its data; an RBAC object that could not be read (a List whose items are not a list, a binding of an unknown API group, a subject that is a string or of a kind the API does not define) opens the RBAC leg; a refused SecurityContextConstraints list opens it for an account that can create pods; and (superseded below) a 404 was read as "not served". The object named is the object scored, for every kind: an old ReplicaSet or a CronJob's Job, not its owner's template; the owner difference names the images and labels that differ. Every list in the document is bounded, not only the graph -- the open legs first, with legsWithheld and resolvedWithheld -- and no input, at any cap the CLI accepts, exceeds the byte cap (400 Ray worker groups gave 500 KB before). OpenShift's legacy authorization.openshift.io Roles and bindings are read as the RBAC objects they proxy (they were dropped), and any other RBAC kind of an unknown group is named and opens the leg. Two different Roles of one name are not read and their union builds no path; two different Services of one name are both followed and the path says which; two different workloads of one name are both scored, and the threat model keeps both (it kept whichever came last; see below). --format mermaid on posture scan now names posture scan and points to posture reach.

posture scan changes again, measured on every YAML under examples/ and tests/fixtures/ and 112 stress files against main. On the repository's own fixtures: seeded.yaml gains the one score-5 path above; cve-probe.yaml and gate/bad/route.yaml name the Ingress or Route whose Service they do not carry; bad-bundle.yaml names a Route of the wrong API group, now ignored; legacy-bundle.yaml counts its v1beta1 Ingress as an exposure (0 to 1, no path); nothing else moves. Every output gains two standing limitations (what exposure is read from; which token is followed). On the stress files, by cause: a hostPath that normalises to /etc (/etc/../etc/) scores 27, not 21, because the scan and the audit's KN-S010 now share one list of node-takeover paths (see the KN-S010 entry above); * resources in the apps group no longer grant exec (pods/exec is core); deployments in a group that does not serve them is not a workload right; OpenShift's scope-impersonation binding no longer ranks a 27; a cluster-admin binding keeps the six paths main gave rather than twelve (rights already inside full control are not listed again); the same Deployment twice is one workload; a duplicate Service no longer doubles a path; the new rights and tokens above add paths where they apply. vuln threat follows the scan: on seeded.yaml 58 threats become 59 and on code/shop.yaml 30 become 31, in each case one TM-E03 for the token-minting right edit carries; on the stress files its rows move exactly where the scan's paths do.

A third review found ten more, all fixed with a test that failed first. A failed live list is never an absence: the round-2 rule that a 404 meant "not served" read an Istio older than 1.22, a Gateway API older than v1.0, an unavailable openshift-apiserver and the phrase inside a warning line as exposure none, complete; kube-noob reads no API discovery, so every failed list, 404 included, is in notRead and its leg is not determined, and the notServed key is gone (cost, read live from a plain Kubernetes cluster: 18 of 25 legs determined on the sets above, from 22). posture snapshot and posture report on a cluster without Gateway API or Istio gain the two failed lists in notRead. Read with --read-namespace, an HTTPRoute or VirtualService in another namespace can route in, so exposure is not determined; in files an unmodelled routing object counts where its backends are. Two different workloads of one name are both kept (their union), so vuln threat keeps a copy's cluster-admin token beside the other copy's hostPID and lists the ambiguity under ambiguous; gate --scanner-report and code assess read the same union. A projected Secret source holding a token Secret is a token, and with automount off an env, envFrom or volume reference to a Secret that may be a token (a token Secret, one the files do not carry, any Secret read live) leaves the token leg open. A VirtualService delegate takes its parent's gateways and an unresolved delegate is a gap; a mirror is a route. cluster_get on an HTTPRoute or VirtualService masks every header value the route sets or matches. The summary follows the exposure leg (a hostPort pod is exposed). Reach names the duplicates the scan names and is not complete over a duplicated binding or front; an exposure field that is not the shape the API requires is not determined; copies differing only in server-set fields (resourceVersion, uid, generation, timestamps, managedFields, status) are one object; a refusal is quoted to a word boundary.

A fourth review found ten more, all fixed with a test that failed on ce5c700 first. An object without a namespace is placed where -n says, as kubectl apply -n does (posture scan -f, posture reach, vuln threat and the MCP reach tool); without -n its namespace is not known, so its token and RBAC legs are not determined and the scan and threat model name it -- it was put in default, and Argo CD's install.yaml read "read Secrets (default)" for an application controller bound to every verb on everything. Two different objects of one name are placed apart, not merged: the round-three union joined one copy's vulnerable image to the other's cluster-admin token in vuln report -m, gate --scanner-report and code assess (a 58.8 where main said 29.4); each copy is now its own reach, and their output carries ambiguous (text and JSON) with each copy's identity. An HTTPRoute's requestMirror backend is a route; a backend that is not a Service (an InferencePool) is not determined; GRPCRoutes are read like HTTPRoutes and listed live (grpcroutes joins the allowlist and the chart's read Role); live, exposure is never none, since TLSRoutes, TCPRoutes, UDPRoutes and other routers are not listed. A live scan of some namespaces says routes elsewhere can route in. A Service without a selector or an ExternalName aliasing an in-cluster Service behind a front, and a Service type that is a list or unknown, are not determined. Controller-set annotations (deployment.kubernetes.io/revision, deprecated.daemonset.template.generation, last-applied) do not make two objects.

A fifth review found six more, all fixed with a test that failed on ef9cdec first. Without -n, a Service, Route, Ingress, HTTPRoute, GRPCRoute or VirtualService with no namespace makes exposure not determined, and a Role or RoleBinding with no namespace makes the RBAC leg not determined, each named; reach and the scan say so in limitations, and a binding to an unplaced Role says so instead of "is not in the input". Copies of one name are ref#1, #2 everywhere, so vuln report counts a finding once per copy (it counted one CVE twice for the name) and the note names the fields that differ. -n reaches vuln report, code report, gate (--namespace, which also renders charts) and the MCP tools, and is checked as a namespace name; an unplaced account or binding is a line in the ranking's ambiguous (at most 20, then a count). More credential carriers are masked: Istio withoutHeaders, haproxy request-/response-set-header annotations, an nginx set $var "<value>", Basic/Bearer credentials and JSON secret fields (clientSecret) in annotation values. An HTTPRoute filters that is not a list is not determined. Only server-set metadata and status are ignored when comparing copies, so a full kubectl get -o yaml copy beside its file is still named as a second object.

  • A read that failed was reported as a thing that is not there, and a live answer was given where something it needed was never read (G279). policy reach --live decided "not installed" by looking for "not found" anywhere in an error, so an auth plugin missing from PATH, a proxy's 404 or kubectl's bare "could not find the requested resource" dropped a tier or the CNI read. An adversarial review then found five more ways the same command answered ALLOWED with exit 0 that it could not stand behind: namespace labels it could not read (an AdminNetworkPolicy subject or a namespaceSelector then matched nothing); a discovery warning about the resource's own group cut off by the 800-character message; an unread CNI compared only "every tier enforced" with "none", while Calico 3.32 enforces ClusterNetworkPolicy and ignores AdminNetworkPolicy and OVN-Kubernetes the reverse; a forbidden Deployment read answered about a Pod of the same name; and items holding non-objects read as zero policies. A user could have been told allowed (exit 0, read by a CI gate) for traffic an unread policy denies, or denied from an AdminNetworkPolicy the cluster ignores. Now one classifier decides for every live reader, from kubectl's own whole line in the full redacted stderr or from the in-cluster client's HTTP status, never from a name or path kube-noob echoed; every failed, partial or malformed read -- a NetworkPolicy list (forbidden is now undetermined naming the namespace, not exit 4), namespace labels a selector reads, a tier, the CNI, a body that is not JSON or UTF-8 -- makes the verdict undetermined with the reason in the text view, JSON and the MCP result; Calico served without a usable version is Calico of unknown version, not "no Calico"; a workload whose first-ranked kind could not be read is an error unless the kind is named (shop/pod/orders); the cluster_version MCP tool, debug, bundle and policy reach --live say platformNotRead instead of calling an unread cluster Kubernetes; the admission lookups keep the last good list per resource (marked stale) instead of reading "none exist". A plain cluster where every CRD is absent answers exactly as before, and the 216 file-mode policy reach -f runs are byte-identical. Measured on the reviewer's matrix (10 read states x 7 resources, plus the CNI under a deciding AdminNetworkPolicy): every non-clean read that changes the answer is now undetermined.

Separately, terminal and file output let invisible characters through. Eleven modules had hand-written control-character lists that missed the Arabic letter mark, the soft hyphen, the tag block and bidi overrides, and the most-used views -- audit text and --compact, validate, gate --github-annotations, the YAML view -- passed raw ESC, OSC 8 and OSC 52 sequences, so a name could move the cursor, hide a link or write the clipboard. Every CLI text write now passes through one boundary (sanitize.visible_lines; documents and the long-running servers excepted, see below), the HTML report and the Markdown writers through the same helper, and a test drives every command the parser has, or names why not. visible follows one documented rule: escapes are removed, other controls become a space, format characters are removed without one, ZWJ and ZWNJ stay after non-ASCII text (Hindi, Sinhala, Malayalam, Persian, emoji sequences), and the result no longer depends on the interpreter's Unicode table. A name holding a lone surrogate ("web\ud800") failed the whole audit with exit 4; it is replaced on input. Output without these characters is byte-identical.

A second review found twelve more, all fixed on the same change. Documents are data again: fix, policy generate, --format yaml|json|sarif went through the text cleaner, so private-use glyphs became spaces and a soft hyphen vanished from a ConfigMap you then applied; they now bypass it, the YAML dumper escapes (double-quoted, lossless) any string that would not print, and stdout is byte-for-byte what -o writes. The admission webhook read a list it had never read as "none exist" and denied a clean Deployment (KN-N001); a cross-object rule over an unread or stale list is now skipped with a warning saying so. The in-cluster backend took any 404 for "not served"; only the API server's exact 404 page not found body is. An unread NetworkPolicy list made the verdict undetermined while the direction said evaluated: true, allowed: false; the direction now says evaluated: false, allowed: null, "could not be read". "No Calico" meant "so OVN-Kubernetes": on Cilium, kindnet, flannel or OpenShift SDN an AdminNetworkPolicy decided answers the CNI may not enforce. The CNI is now read as Cilium when CiliumClusterwideNetworkPolicy is served and as OVN-Kubernetes when EgressFirewall is, and otherwise "was not identified", which leaves an answer the admin tier decides undetermined (--cni still wins); on the reviewer's 32 profile rows (kind, OpenShift OVN, Calico 3.30, Cilium x admin and namespace-scoped) no verdict changed, because none of them has an admin-tier policy deciding. A list response must now be a List or <Kind>List, every item named, in the namespace asked for, and on one page (metadata.continue unset), or it is a failed read; a policyTypes that is not a list is undetermined. Unicode: escape sequences now become nothing (a space split the word they sat inside), a removed character between two identifier characters is shown as <U+200B> so web<U+3164>x is not webx, Hangul fillers, CGJ, variation selectors not after an emoji-capable character, Mongolian selectors, unassigned default-ignorables, noncharacters and the Braille blank are handled, and combining runs are capped at four; clean() (MCP) and visible() share one implementation, and MCP text content goes through it. The servers serve, mcp and admission serve are not wrapped, because their stdout is a protocol stream. The structural test that forbids private control-character lists reads decoded constants, raw strings, \N{}, f-strings, bytes, filter(str.isprintable), getattr, aliased category and range(0x80, 0xa0) (19 of the reviewer's 19 evasions caught, 5 of 5 innocent cases pass), and on 63 MB visible_lines costs 0.25 s ASCII and 0.97 s non-ASCII, 0.43 s and 1.18 s with the workflow-command guard below. zstd's stderr is captured and reported, not inherited. Not changed, by the owner's decision: undetermined exits 1, shared with denied.

A third review found more. watch was left unwrapped with the servers although it prints text views; it is wrapped, and only serve, mcp and admission serve are not. admission review printed its AdmissionReview through the text boundary, which changed the document; documents now never pass it. An escape sequence between two word characters is shown as <ESC> (web<ESC>x is not webx), and still removed in prose. An error kube-noob did not anticipate printed a raw traceback and exited 1, which a pipeline reads as findings; it is one cleaned line and exit 4, with the traceback under KUBE_NOOB_DEBUG=1. A live list answer with no kind (a misrouted endpoint's {"items": []}) is a failed read, not "none exist". Offline, policy reach -f without --cni keeps evaluating the admin tiers with OVN-Kubernetes semantics (G207), and now says so in the text and the JSON notes. The structural test also catches an aliased unicodedata, ord(c) in range(...), translate tables built over a range, string.printable, bytes([27]).decode(), chr(int("1b", 16)) and codecs.decode(..., "unicode_escape"), with no false failure in src. Known limitation, documented in SECURITY.md: on the in-cluster backend, Go reverse proxies (Traefik, kube-rbac-proxy) send the same 404 page not found body as the API server.

A fourth review found two more in the shared pieces. sanitize.visible() removed a format character or an escape sequence between two name characters unless the caller asked for markers, and eight callers did not, so openshift<ZWSP>-etcd reached Alertmanager signals, the operator inventory, must-gather analysis, tickets, code SARIF and policy explain as openshift-etcd; and the terminal printer cleaned without markers before the output boundary could mark. Marking is now the default everywhere, the MCP cleaner included; without markers a removed character inside a name leaves a space, never nothing; the same set of name characters (._:/ included) decides for escapes and format characters, so api<ESC>.internal is not api.internal; and one_line is idempotent (a Unicode space was taken for the base of a joiner, and two combining runs could join past the cap). A document written into a pipe whose reader left mid-way (audit --format json | head -c 10) exited 1, or 0 for fix and policy generate, with a truncated document: the error was swallowed at the final flush. Every document writer now writes in 64 KiB pieces and flushes inside the command, so it exits 141 like the text view.

  • Security: a name in a manifest could run a CI workflow command in the step that printed it (G279, review CP3-Q3). The runner reads a command from every line a step writes -- ::name:: at the start of a line, and the legacy ##[name] ANYWHERE in it (actions/runner v2.321.0, ActionCommand.TryParse) -- and kube-noob printed ConfigMap keys, names and pasted text as they were. A key p##[stop-commands]token in an audit line stopped command processing for the rest of the step; ##[error file=...]All checks passed forged an annotation; ##[set-output name=result]passed set a step output; ##[add-mask]KN-S026 hid text. docs/use-cases.md tells users to run kube-noob audit --fail-on high in CI. Azure Pipelines (##vso[task.setvariable variable=KUBECONFIG]...) and TeamCity (##teamcity[...]) read theirs anywhere in a line too. Every text write, stdout and stderr, every command but the three servers, now goes through sanitize.CommandGuard at the output boundary, always -- not only under GITHUB_ACTIONS and not only off a terminal, because a log is replayed and the same input must give the same bytes: the bracket of ##[, ##vso[ or ##teamcity[ is shown as <U+005B> and the first colon of a line-leading :: as <U+003A>, the marker style kube-noob already uses for characters it removes, so the change cannot be mistaken for text. The comment header policy generate writes, which echoed values from the exceptions file (scope.namespace: "mon##[endgroup]" was a parsed endgroup in stdout and in the -o file), and the Markdown reports are broken the same way. Documents stay lossless: JSON writes the bracket as \u005b, YAML double-quotes on one line only a string holding such a prefix or a :: a line could start with (so listen [::]:80; keeps its block style), and the SARIF, Code Quality and HTML files gate writes, the AdmissionReview admission review prints, the debug bundle and the Helm chart files are treated the same. The annotations gate --github-annotations writes on purpose are the only commands left: their text is cleaned, then escaped, and cannot carry another. Tested with the runner's two parsers ported from its source; the reviewer's fuzzers found none in 100 000 stream cases and in 10 000 end-to-end payloads through 16 commands, with Azure and TeamCity payloads added.

  • The Alertmanager receiver answered with names, and the north-star number was a sentence (G274). POST /v1/alerts returned the signals a payload carried and kept nothing, so "alert to evidence pack in a minute" could be neither delivered nor measured. POST /v1/alert-reports now takes Alertmanager's own payload and answers with one evidence report per alert: the signals, the diagnosis the playbooks give, the reported OpenShift issues for the release that alert's own labels name, approved team memory when configured, and read-only next steps -- each from the diagnose tool called with that alert alone, not a second implementation of it. Every section says what it could not determine: no version label, a release outside the ones the local issues cover (not evaluated rather than "none"), no playbook, no memory configured, a memory repository that could not be read (not evaluated rather than "searched, nothing found"). An alert kube-noob recognises nothing in is reported as exactly that; a resolved alert is recorded, not diagnosed; an alert whose diagnosis fails, or that the time budget did not reach, keeps a not-evaluated row; list items that are not alerts and alerts past 500 are counted. Names in the suggested commands come only from the alert's own labels and only when they are valid Kubernetes names, never from annotation prose -- and plain diagnose on an Alertmanager payload had the same hole, so it reads names the same way now. Label and annotation values are redacted before they are clipped (a private key cut by the clip used to survive) and their scheme:// URLs become [url removed]; the shared redactor also catches Basic credentials, a key named by a word and a space, live and test API keys and a private key with no END line. The body is byte-stable for a payload reported within its budget; the id, times and duration sit outside it.

An adversarial review found that one in-bounds post of long dash runs took 43-62 s and slowed /healthz to half a second: the redactor's flag pattern was tried at every dash. It now runs only near a keyword, rewriting exactly what it rewrote before (checked against the old substitution on 24,000 generated strings), which took the worst case from 40.4 s to 4.5 s on an idle 2-core build machine. A post also gets alertReports.budgetSeconds (30) of work, after which the remaining alerts keep not-evaluated rows and are counted; and the body is read, the report built and the answer rendered in worker threads, two reports at a time, so a large post no longer holds the event loop. A size cap that cut the most severe alert entirely now keeps a row for every alert and takes detail from resolved and low-severity alerts first, naming them. One lone surrogate in one label used to reject the whole group with 400.

With alertReports.store the server keeps each report in <dataDir>/alert-reports (owner-only, written atomically and never through a link, the newest alertReports.maxReports for at most maxAgeDays, each bounded by maxReportBytes), and GET /v1/alert-reports and GET /v1/alert-reports/<id> read them back through the same bearer, Host/Origin and tool path as every route, type-checked down to each alert row and cleaned again on the way out; a damaged one is a 500, not a refusal of the caller's input, and a FIFO planted at a report's name is refused instead of blocking the read. Retention never removes the report just written, and something it cannot remove under a report's name is counted rather than stopping it for good; listing and reading change nothing on disk. Storage off, or a data directory that cannot be written, is stored: false with the reason on the POST, never a silent loss. The chart keeps none by default: its two replicas each have a volume, so a report stored by one was not found through the Service on the other, and store: true now needs replicaCount: 1. The receipt-to-report time is measured for every payload, returned, kept and exported as kube_noob_alert_report_duration_seconds, with a dashboard row; kube-noob demo alerts posts the bundled payload and fails over a 2 s budget. kube-noob alerts report -f builds the same report offline with no server; alerts list and alerts show read what a server kept and exit 4, not with a traceback, on a damaged file. Not built, recorded in the roadmap: forwarding a report to chat or ITSM, enriching it from the cluster, and caller identity.

A second adversarial review found what those fixes broke or left open. Two were regressions shared by every tool that redacts text (diagnose, insight, the must-gather report, build logs, incidents, the bundle and the learning-note filter): a lone -----BEGIN ... PRIVATE KEY----- line deleted everything after it, so a log line quoting the header ("expected -----BEGIN RSA PRIVATE KEY----- in key.pem") erased the pod name and the rest of the log; and the new api-key pattern redacted English ("rotate the apikey whenever a teammate leaves", "Authorization: Basic realm missing") and made the note filter refuse it. A cut key now loses its base64 body and nothing else, a key needs a value that looks like one, and a Basic value goes whole or not at all. The private-key and JWT patterns, which ran each attempt to the end of the text, are scans that look at each character a bounded number of times: 64 KB of -----BEGIN A PRIVATE KEY----- went from 1.2 s to 8 ms and of eyJ from 1.5 s to 5 ms, and a 400 KB eyJ text through diagnose from 55 s to 1.3 s. The redactor also catches client_secret=, aws_secret_access_key=, DB_PASSWORD=-style names, a bare Bearer token, a key file's "private_key" and --pwd, and PRIVACY.md now lists what it removes and what it does not.

The receiver read and parsed each post before waiting for its turn, with no bound on the wait, so 120 posts the sender had given up on held their bodies (the server at 410 MB against the chart's 512Mi) and were still built and stored afterwards; the MCP tool did not share the two-at-a-time limit at all. One set of slots now serves the receiver and the tool; a post is answered 503 with Retry-After after 5 s without one (an MCP call gets a busy tool error), and is dropped unbuilt and unstored if its sender has gone; the time budget counts from receipt, so a wait is part of it rather than added to it; large request bodies waiting in memory are capped at eight (the third review below changed how). kube_noob_alert_reports_dropped_total counts both refusals, and the duration histogram, whose top bucket was 60 s, now runs to 600 s. Kube-state-metrics' service label named the service in commands for every pod alert it reports; a scrape target's service is now ignored. Label names replaced every name read from the text, so a forbidden message's verb and user, a pull secret and a claim were lost; labels now set only the namespace and pod. diagnose and the report read the release label in different orders; both now take the alert's openshift_version, then the group's, then the alert's version, then the group's. A directory planted under a report's name was listed as unreadable forever and pushed the list past maxReports, and a hard link to a file outside the store was served; entries that are not regular files are now ignored and counted, and a file with a second link is refused. The safety contract also fails an unreviewed Literal, URL or non-empty list default and a default-on boolean inside a list of models, which it used to pass.

A third review found that those server-wide limits could be held by the wrong requests. A report slot was taken before the body was read, with no read deadline, so two senders trickling a byte at a time held both slots and every real alert post was refused as "busy" -- with a message claiming two reports were being built -- for as long as they trickled; the large-body cap counted declared lengths, so eight trickling posts refused every large post on every route, and it refused rather than queued the ninth and tenth of ten ordinary parallel 320 KB audits. Every body must now arrive within 10 s (408 otherwise), a body counts as large only once it is in memory, a request that finds no room waits for it until its read deadline, and a large body's place is freed as soon as its tool takes it (an alert report: as soon as it holds a report slot, which it now takes after reading the body). A report slot is taken on the event loop for MCP calls too: waiting for it inside a worker thread let sixty MCP reports push a small diagnose to a p95 of 6.7 s (now a p50 of 73 ms). A refusal on /mcp is now a JSON-RPC error the MCP SDK shows with its message, instead of a plain body it reported as "Server returned an error response". Every POST route documents 408, 413, 499 and 503. On the labels: an exporter's own pod and namespace (node-exporter, kube-state-metrics on a node alert in a monitoring namespace) are no longer the subject, exported_namespace/exported_pod are when the plain labels name the exporter, TargetDown names its target service again, and an application's version="4.2" is no longer read as OpenShift 4.2 (only openshift_version, or version on a platform component's alert). The redactor leaves a reference to a Secret (<set to the key 'password' in secret 'orders-db'>) alone, catches --access-key, and removes a cut key's body through log prefixes, JSON-escaped newlines and a key flattened onto one line; the safety contract also sees through dict and Optional models, validators, any non-first Literal choice and a bare host name.

A fourth review found that the third's exemption for a <name>/<key> value leaked a real password of that shape (password=prod-db/secret, DB_PASSWORD=mysupersecret/key). The exemption now needs the surrounding text to prove a reference -- kubectl describe's <set to the key '…' in secret '…'>, secret '…' key '…', a manifest's valueFrom/secretKeyRef -- and a plain assignment is redacted again, over-redacting DB_PASSWORD: orders-db/password rather than leaking. An exported_pod/exported_namespace label is now the subject whatever the exporter is called: the prefix only exists because a scrape without honorLabels collided with the target's own label.

/v1/reports is unchanged: it is the daily posture report, and a list of alert reports under it would have made one public path answer two questions. The OpenAPI document (now 1.1.0) describes the two Alertmanager routes' bodies as Alertmanager's payload rather than as a {"text": ...} argument map they never took, and lists the 403, 405 and 421 every route can answer. A method a shared path does not serve now gets an Allow header naming every method that is. The safety contract's comment promised that a boolean defaulting to on needed a reviewed line, and nothing checked it: --check now fails on one that is not listed (it surfaced mustGather.enabled, now classified as a local file read rather than an "opt-in feature"), and its sentence that only tools that are not read-only write the learning file was wrong -- read-only tools bump its counters. Separately, doc_numbers.py measured "red-team scenarios" in the README from the demo list, which also numbered nine until this change added a demo. - Whether the release notes report a problem with an installed operator could not be asked, and the obvious answer would have been wrong (R5, G278). The inventory knew each operator's package and version and the bundled release notes knew 921 issues, but nothing joined them, and the join R5 proposed -- installed operator version against fixed version -- has nothing to join on: no release-note record carries a component, a package or an operator version, and every fix is an OpenShift release.

kube-noob operators issues (the same -f, --must-gather and --live inputs and collection as operators inventory), the operator_known_issues and cluster_operator_known_issues tools, and operatorKnownIssues in must-gather analyze and analyze_must_gather are a release-note lookup, not a vulnerability check and not a gate. Each installed operator's package goes through a table of the names the notes use: the product's name (named) or a component name or abbreviation (mentioned, a weaker tier, never merged), and an issue that also names another product or a platform component is a mention with that reason. Open issues come first, and a known issue with no fix stays listed at later releases. A fix is never decided for the operator: fixReleaseVsCluster compares the listed OpenShift release with the cluster's version only. Operators from community-operators, certified-operators or redhat-marketplace are not looked up, and any other catalog is labelled unverified. The cluster's version is read from the ClusterVersion named version even when one is given (a disagreement is reported); an update in progress searches both releases, given a version or not; a cluster that does not serve ClusterVersion is not OpenShift, and an OKD version is not OpenShift Container Platform. --require-complete exits 5 when the evidence was not complete, including a cluster the release notes do not cover (not OpenShift, OKD, a release outside the index), and names the reason; whether every operator was matched is allMatched in the JSON, not an exit code.

An adversarial review of the first version found 15 defects, all fixed here. Thirty operators with no table entry read assessed and passed --require-complete; an open issue from 4.20 vanished at 4.21; a cluster without OpenShift, or a community build, got OpenShift bugs; 17 PTP and 14 Service Mesh records were invisible and the rows shown were mostly fixed ones; every fix carried a reason that was false for console and ingress-operator bugs; a refreshed local corpus that dropped a name read as "named in none"; an update in progress was complete; a given version silently beat the cluster's; truncation kept fixed rows over open ones and sorted ids as text; the must-gather embedding pointed at flags that do not exist; the text lacked fields the JSON had; and a two-ClusterVersion must-gather used whichever came first. The known-issue loader, shared with known-issues, diagnose and the must-gather report, now checks every record of a local copy (issue keys, ASCII-digit versions, release-notes sources, dates) and leaves out, counts and names any that fail; the bundled snapshot loads unchanged. scripts/vendor_known_issues.py --check fails on a record field the loader does not know and prints how many records each name hits.

A second review found the headline and the numbers beside it still disagreeing, all fixed here. allMatched was true with nothing looked up (no installed operator, or no OLM at all), because zero not-matched is vacuously all; it is now true only for the matched verdict. complete and incompleteBecause were computed separately and disagreed three ways (no OLM with an unreadable ClusterVersion: complete with a reason; not OpenShift and a release outside the index: incomplete with no reason); complete is now exactly not incompleteBecause, those two cases carry their reason, and a release reason is not added when there is nothing to look up. --require-matched reused exit 5, which means unread evidence, for a package missing from kube-noob's own name table -- a coverage gap -- and is removed; jq -e '.allMatched == true' is the gate for anyone who wants one. A known issue whose listed fix is in a later release's z-stream (known in 4.20, fixed in 4.21.3) vanished on a 4.21.1 cluster that does not have the fix yet. OKD (4.20.0-okd-scos.6) was read as OpenShift and answered from OpenShift's release notes. A must-gather whose ClusterVersion is not named version flipped to "a Kubernetes cluster" with "no ClusterVersion collected" and lost the ClusterVersion's condition findings. releasesSearched listed a release nothing was searched in, and two caveats about one update contradicted each other. partly-matched beside zero not-matched did not say every match came from a catalog kube-noob cannot verify. --openshift-version equal to the completed version hid an update in progress. The text view now shows complete and every id of a row, the must-gather hint names the flag that shows more issues, the tool refuses "4.21\n" as the CLI does, a valid local release-notes copy older than the bundled one says why it was not used, and an operator whose install was not read is never named. The must-gather report had the same OKD blind spot as its neighbour: report.md, report.json, case-draft.md and the must-gather analyze text titled an OKD cluster "OpenShift 4.20.0-okd-…" and matched its findings against OpenShift's release notes. They now decide OKD exactly as operators issues --must-gather does, say OKD, and report the known-issue lookup as knownIssuesVerdict: not-determined with the same reason (knownIssuesNote); not-applicable for a cluster with no ClusterVersion, looked-up otherwise. The text view also prints installedHasFix: no-fix-listed for a row with no fix, as the JSON does.

  • Every link and command a user copies still named the account the project moved out of (G275). G266 repointed the references the move broke; GitHub redirects the rest, so they kept working and nothing failed -- they just told every reader to type an owner the project no longer has: the README's badges, social-preview image and doc links, brew tap ... https://github.com/<previous owner>/kube-noob, /plugin marketplace add <previous owner>/kube-noob, the uses: line for the GitHub Action, the GitLab template's include URL, the PyPI project URLs, the image labels, the chart's home, sources and support URL, the formula's homepage, the MCP server's website_url, the HTTP User-Agent and the knowledge sources. And each new file was copied from an old one, so it would have carried the old owner on. 88 occurrences in 35 files now name kube-noob; the Homebrew formula and the plugin's server copy were regenerated by their scripts rather than edited.

The occurrences that stay are a classification, not leftovers, and each one is in an allowlist with its reason: people (CODEOWNERS, the OpenShift catalog OWNERS file and the fork a catalog pull request comes from), artifacts that really live under the previous owner, history, and the guards that refuse it. Everything published before the move stays where it was, because a signature cannot be moved: images and charts 0.7.1 and earlier are in the previous owner's GHCR namespace and their wheels' provenance names the previous repository, so SECURITY.md now gives a separate cosign verify for those and points at PyPI's provenance for their wheels -- the repository's attestation API answers 404 for a 0.7.1 wheel, so the command it gave could not verify them. docs/install.md gained a row for anyone who tapped Homebrew under the old name. A test now fails when any tracked file names the previous owner outside that allowlist, or when an allowlist entry stops matching, so the next file added cannot bring it back. - Which operators are installed was a question kube-noob could only half answer, and an input it had not fully read would have answered it anyway (E2, G270). The must-gather analyser kept a name, a namespace and a phase per ClusterServiceVersion and folded every CSV of the same name into one (that summary, cluster.operators in must-gather analyze, is unchanged), and there was no way to ask a cluster or a set of manifests at all. kube-noob operators inventory (-f, --must-gather, --live), the operator_inventory and cluster_operator_inventory tools, and operatorInventory in must-gather analyze (text and JSON) and in analyze_must_gather now say, per operator: package, version and where the version came from (spec.version, the CSV's olm.package property, or derived-from-name, labelled), catalog, channel, approval mode, namespace, the namespaces it serves, phase (unknown when absent or not one OLM defines, and counted as not Succeeded), a pending upgrade and a Manual approval waiting.

An adversarial review of the first version found 21 ways it answered listed, complete or installed for an input it had not fully read, and all are fixed. A Subscription is joined on status.installedCSV alone, to a CSV that is an original in its own namespace, so a copy never stands in for a missing install, an incoming currentCSV is never reported as the installed version, and two Subscriptions naming one CSV give one operator; a Subscription with no status is status-not-read, not "not installed". Copies (olm.copiedFrom, reason Copied) fold into their original only when their version and phase match it. The verdict is listed only when nothing was skipped: a file in a directory that could not be parsed, a must-gather file the reader skipped (a link, a file over its size cap), an OLM file outside the layout oc adm inspect writes, an empty file, a file holding an error or a Status, a list item that is not an object of its kind, objects past the read cap, an object of another API group (an ACM or Knative Subscription, now ignored), duplicates that disagree, and a namespace with no Subscription in a file set each make it partial and are named. no-olm needs a 404 in the exact words the backends write for a missing resource type on both reads -- an auth plugin "not found" used to read as "not an OLM cluster" -- and a live response with no items list is a failed read. Operator CSVs named like secrets-store-* were dropped by the must-gather reader as Secret data; files in the OLM layout are no longer subject to that name rule. --require-complete exits 5 on anything but listed or no-olm. The output is the same bytes for the same objects in any order. Shown strings lose every non-printing character by Unicode category through sanitize.visible, which the must-gather analysers now use too. The count of dropped Secret objects is droppedUnread.

The read-only allowlist gains clusterserviceversions and subscriptions (operators.coreos.com/v1alpha1, namespaced), with the chart's read Role granting both; cluster-wide, copies are excluded with the !olm.copiedFrom selector OLM itself uses. InstallPlans and OperatorGroups are read from files only. The must-gather reader is not in the Claude plugin; the inventory over manifests is. The output keys package and version are what R5 will match against the known-issue corpus.

  • audit --format sarif, with the controls on the rules and the run's blind spots in the file (G269). SARIF is a list of results, so a partial audit and a clean one differ only in length. The audit's assumptions (what it did not evaluate, for example because it was given part of a namespace) and its unreadable files now travel in the document as toolExecutionNotifications, where the JSON report already carried them and a format that replaces the report would have dropped them.

Every rule in the audit's SARIF and the gate's carries its NIST SP 800-53 controls, as NIST-800-53/<id> tags and as a nist-800-53 list; a rule mapped to none has no such property. --format sarif on any command but audit exits 2 rather than printing YAML under the name, and --oscal with --format sarif exits 2 rather than choosing for you. The audit's exit code is unchanged by the flag. The SARIF informationUri names the project's current home. This completes I2.

  • The SARIF the gate hands to code scanning named a schema nothing had ever read (G268). Every file gate --sarif writes carries "$schema": "https://json.schemastore.org/sarif-2.1.0.json", and no test or CI step had ever validated a document against it, in a tool whose argument is that it needs no network. It was valid; it was valid by luck.

The OASIS schema is now vendored under tests/fixtures/sarif/ with its upstream sha256, refetched by python scripts/vendor_schemas.py --sarif, and both scripts/check.sh and CI validate the gate's own output against it. SARIF 2.1.0 declares draft-04 and uses the id keyword that ajv 8 refuses, so the validator now picks its compiler from the dialect the schema declares instead of rewriting the schema; a test asserts that dialect so a re-vendor cannot fall through to the wrong one, and another requires the validator to reject a document with a level the schema does not allow, since a validator that waves everything through passes as quietly as one that works. scripts/oscal_validate.mjs is now scripts/json_schema_validate.mjs: one validator, two dialects.

  • The plugin directory refuses the plugin, and stripping the server out of it would have shipped a lie (G267). The Claude plugin directory held plugin/kube-noob with two findings, both gated on a Verified-organization label with no application path: local MCP servers and files outside a short allow-list are accepted only from Verified organizations. The plugin is 218 files, 99 of them a refused type, and it declares a local stdio server, so there is no edit that keeps the server and passes.

plugin/kube-noob is unchanged and still installs from this repository's own marketplace with every tool. A second plugin, plugin/kube-noob-skills, carries the same knowledge as skills alone for the directory. Its seven skills were rewritten rather than copied: the originals call 46 kubenoob_* MCP tools, and a plugin with no server that still names them would be an absence reading as a pass. Each one now says which step is reasoning rather than verification, and ends with what it cannot do and the CLI command that can. A test fails the build if kubenoob_ appears in that folder, if a file is of a type the directory refuses, if a skill is added to one plugin and not the other, or if a kube-noob command line in a skill names a subcommand or flag the CLI does not define -- which five of the first drafts did.

Both plugin manifests and the plugin folder's README also still named the previous owner after the organisation move, which the directory renders as the listing. The test that should have caught it pinned the old owner as a literal, so it asserted the staleness rather than refusing it; it now refuses any of the fields the directory shows naming that account.

  • docs/open-questions.md: the decisions this project has not made, written for an external reviewer. ideation-review.md records what three external reviews proposed and what was refused; this is its forward-facing twin. It carries what the thing is in ten lines, the non-negotiables (so a reviewer does not spend effort on proposals that will be refused), the plan as it stands with the moat reconciliation (21 shipped, 7 partial, 29 not started), every open proposal with what already exists for it, and five numbered questions.

The questions are the point, because the last round produced about 250 proposals and five survivors, which suggests coverage is not the constraint: whether cryptography is worth it for R2, whether caller identity (N13) should outrank the whole moat queue, whether I1 or M6 is the right thing to prove first, where the line is on an unknown never reading as a pass before it becomes noise a reader ignores, and what is missing from every list. Every number in it was read off the code at v0.9.2 rather than recalled.

0.9.2 - 2026-11-08

  • The chart installed an image that does not exist, and four other things the repository move broke (G266). kube-noob moved from a personal account into the kube-noob organisation.
