GitHub redirects the old repository URLs, so most references kept working and the breakage stayed invisible until each step ran. The ones that do not redirect: GHCR namespaces, the MCP registry namespace, cosign certificate identities, gh attestation --repo, and the site repositories.

Chart 0.9.1 is published and signed and defaults image.repository to the old namespace, while the release pushes to the new one -- so helm install with defaults ends in ImagePullBackOff. The Tekton Task, the GitLab template and the sidecar manifest hard-coded the same image. SECURITY.md told users to verify the supply chain with gh attestation verify --repo and a cosign identity that name a repository which no longer owns the attestations, so our own verification instructions could not succeed. CI could never have caught the chart defect: gate-ci built the image locally and tagged it with the old name before loading it into kind, so the test passed by minting the very thing it should have been checking for.

server.json and the mcp-name marker in README.md now both read io.github.kube-noob/kube-noob. That pair could only take effect in a new release: the registry reads the marker from the published PyPI description, and 0.9.1's is immutable, which is why publish-mcp-registry could not be fixed by a re-run.

Outside the repository and fixed by hand: the GHCR image and chart were private under the new organisation and are now public, and organisation policy had disabled deploy keys, which is what stopped the website publishing for twelve consecutive runs.

0.9.1 - 2026-11-07

  • 0.9.0 could not have published even with a green attestation (G265). The pinned PyPI publish action runs twine check dist/* and exec twine upload dist/* over the whole directory, and twine 7.0.0 rejects sbom.spdx.json ("Unknown distribution format") and the krew archive ("No PKG-INFO in archive"), aborting before it opens a connection. Both had been copied into dist/ since 2026-11-05, only so the GitHub release would attach them. v0.7.1 shipped before that change, v0.8.0 died on a missing PyYAML and v0.9.0 on a Rekor read-after-write 404 -- so publish-pypi had never once run with those files there. Reproduced with the action's exact twine before changing anything: check and upload both exit 1.

Two blind spots hid it. scripts/check_publishable.sh is the deliberate twin of the action's own check, down to the action SHA and the bundled twine, and it ran before the two steps that contaminated dist/. And ci.yml's publishable job builds the dists without ever running the SBOM or krew steps, so no pull request could see it: that sequence existed only in release.yml and was first executed on a tag.

Non-distribution assets now go to assets/, which github-release attaches and both attestation attempts cover -- krew's manifest points at that archive as a release asset, so it has to reach the release page. check_publishable.sh runs last, over the dist/ the action will actually see. Provenance is attempted twice with a pause, because v0.9.0 failed on Rekor returning 404 for an entry it had just written while every artefact was already correct; the retry still decides the job, so nothing public is ever unattested.

  • scripts/check.sh ran eleven fewer gates than it claimed (G264). It is what AGENTS.md tells you to run after any change to src/, and its header said it ran "every gate CI runs on code". It ran pytest -q where CI runs pytest --cov --cov-fail-under=80, so the coverage floor was never enforced locally, and it never built the wheel, installed and drove it, ran e2e_mcp_offline.py, check_publishable.sh, uv lock --check, either pip-audit, e2e_plugin.py, brew_formula.py --check, or the OSCAL schema validation and its did-it-skip guard. It passed four times running while CI failed.

All of those run now, and a tool this machine lacks is printed at the end under NOT RUN with the words "this is not a pass" instead of being passed over -- helm is the only entry here, because the chart guards have no helm-free path. tests/test_check_sh.py reads ci.yml and fails if a gate a code job runs is neither in check.sh nor named with a reason, which caught two holes in check.sh and one in its own first version. Running check.sh now takes about three times as long, which is the point.

0.9.0 - 2026-11-07

  • Two timing assertions measured the runner instead of the code (G263). test_a_padded_object_does_not_outrun_the_webhooks_timeout asserted elapsed < 5.0 for 4,000 containers; it measured 5.44 s on one shared 3.11 runner and held the 0.9.0 release, while 3.12, 3.13 and 3.14 passed the same code. That object costs 0.86 s here, so the budget had no headroom on a slow machine. The test is named for a shape and its docstring says the fix makes the cost linear, so it now times 1,000 and 4,000 back to back and asserts the growth stays under 8x -- quadratic in 4x the input is about 16x, linear about 4x, and a slow runner slows both halves alike. With G252's memo disabled the ratio is 15.7x and the assertion fires, so it still catches what the budget caught. A warm-up call comes first, because the cold call pays for imports. test_the_memory_demo_runs_offline_under_a_second had the same shape and keeps only a ceiling a hang would cross, with its real contract in the assertions on what the demo printed.

  • A corpus shard bigger than the bundle allows is written as parts. redhat-vex.jsonl is 2.78 GB, and db-bundle refuses a member over 300 MB, so the scheduled db image could not be built. The cap stays. A shard over 250 MB is <name>.jsonl, <name>-2.jsonl, and so on. A read concatenates them. A later write that fits in one file deletes the parts it no longer uses (G262).

  • The plugin and the wheel pin pydantic 2.14.2. 2.14.1 dropped JSON keys that matched an unused alias, and mishandled extra data on a TypedDict (GHSA-qpmf-fgh3-ff25, GHSA-3r8g-cqwr-jr29). The plugin lock moved and the skill install hint still said 2.14.1, so the bump could not merge. The hint, the plugin lock and requirements.lock now say 2.14.2.

  • policy reach against a live cluster no longer treats a Cilium or Calico cluster policy as absent. A pasted CiliumClusterwideNetworkPolicy or Calico GlobalNetworkPolicy was already undetermined. The live read never listed them, so the same objects on the cluster came back allowed and exit 0. Both are read once, cluster-scoped, from the published CRD (cilium.io/v2 ciliumclusterwidenetworkpolicies, crd.projectcalico.org/v1 globalnetworkpolicies). A 404 is "not installed". A 403 is not. The first read-only check is the get that lists that object. A namespace CiliumNetworkPolicy is still not read and still does not flip a pod-to-pod NetworkPolicy answer. The chart's cluster-scoped read role grants get and list for those two resources and nothing else new.

  • A cluster-scoped policy this tool does not read, and a hostNetwork direction, are no longer a false allow. CiliumClusterwideNetworkPolicy and Calico GlobalNetworkPolicy in the input join ClusterNetworkPolicy: the verdict is undetermined and the exit is 1, because either can reverse a namespace answer. An EgressFirewall, a namespace CiliumNetworkPolicy and a MultiNetworkPolicy stay on the "not evaluated" list and do not flip pod-to-pod traffic; they do not decide that traffic. A hostNetwork direction is unknown. It does not erase a deny that AdminNetworkPolicy or NetworkPolicy already returned, including a deny of that same pod. The summary says the NetworkPolicies would deny only when a direction they evaluated actually denied.

  • kube-noob gate -f on a Helm values file matches audit. A file named on the command line used to be audited anyway, so gate -f values.yaml reported KN-V001 (missing apiVersion). It now refuses with the same "nothing here is a Kubernetes manifest" error, exit 2. A directory walk still skips that file. A real manifest beside it still proceeds. A manifest-only gate still passes when Containerfile, vulnerability and code checks were not asked for.

  • helm template is given the Kubernetes version the caller already named. helm-audit and kube-noob gate --chart passed no --kube-version, so Helm 4.0.0 rendered .Capabilities.KubeVersion as v1.20.0. The flag is set only when --version was passed, and an OpenShift version uses the existing release map. No version still means no flag.

  • Install text matches the installer. Python is 3.11.4, and the skill runner in a checkout is skill/kube-noob/scripts/kn.py. The container example stays on the 0.7 tag the release publishes. The plugin instructions name Containerfile and SARIF tools only when that build still has them. The GitLab sample says why it cannot write when the job directory is not writable by uid 1001.

  • A peer kube-noob cannot check no longer lets a later rule allow the traffic. An AdminNetworkPolicy networks or nodes peer used to be skipped after a note, so a following Allow was allowed and exit 0. A CIDR is matched when the other side is an address, and 0.0.0.0/0 or ::/0 matches a pod whose IP is not in the manifests. Anything else that might match is undetermined. A priority above 99 is not evaluated: OVN-Kubernetes does not enforce it. A forbidden read of the admin tiers is not "the tier is not installed"; the namespace-scoped chart cannot read them, and that used to come back as a NetworkPolicy allow (G183).

  • A Jira run that cannot decrypt the previous main copy no longer starts a new one. The decrypt failure used to exit 0 and fetch from scratch, so the first green run after a passphrase mismatch became the artifact every later run restored, and the previous copy stopped being the main copy. The run now fails, the previous artifact is left where it is, and only fresh=true discards it on purpose. The upload also refuses a snapshot with fewer tickets than the copy it restored. The forward pass asks Jira ORDER BY updated DESC and, if the request budget runs out, resumes that same query from the page it had not applied, so a later run does not skip updates and does not write the same ticket twice (G182). The scheduled default is every other day (JIRA_EVERY_DAYS=1 for daily). Ticket text is still not published: it is queried locally with kube-noob ticket after corpus import.

  • Both red scheduled workflows were diagnosed before the VEX member was sharded. db-image in ci.yml was never the failing job. The scheduled db-image.yml is the one that downloads the VEX feed, and that member is now parts (G262). corpus-refresh remains a privacy gate (G179, G182).

  • CHECKED_THROUGH and the risk review's drift baseline are now held together by a test. Both said Kubernetes 1.37, by the coincidence of having been edited the same day, and nothing connected them: raising the removed-API ceiling without the baseline would have produced a drift report that contradicts the data, and raising the baseline alone would mark a version current that the tables have never been read against. Kubernetes 1.38 is released on 16 December 2026, and both now move in one commit or the suite fails (G181).

  • The weekly corpus refresh has been refusing the copy since 2026-10-31, and the failure could not be acted on. The privacy audit exits 1 rather than keep Jira text with person-shaped content in it -- correct, and the reason it is a gate -- but it reported only 123 person-shaped findings: ['CNV-95960: spelled-out email', 'CNV-85843: account id', ...]. The ticket and the kind, nothing more, so separating a real leak from a false positive needed the encrypted copy and the passphrase, and the workflow stayed red for days. Printing the match would be strictly worse: that writes personal data into a public CI log. Each finding now carries the field it came from and a character-class shape of what matched, with no content kept: CNV-95960 text: spelled-out email A at A.A, CNV-85843 text: account id 9A9A9A9. Letters collapse to A and digits to 9, while at and dot survive because they are what makes a spelled-out email recognisable. docs/operations.md now lists the two known false-positive shapes -- a sha256: digest fragment matching BARE_ACCOUNT_ID, and a labelled commenter name before a hostname -- and says that anything else is a real leak to fix in the anonymiser rather than a reason to raise the gate. The patterns themselves are deliberately left broad: over-matching a digest costs a digest, under-matching costs someone's identity (G179).

  • The same audit manufactured findings at a seam of its own making. It scanned text + " " + title, two fields that are sanitised separately, so a pattern could match across the join and refuse a copy over text present in neither -- "escalated to Commenter A at" and "example.com upgrade notes" are each clean and read as a spelled-out email when concatenated. Each field is scanned on its own, and the field is named in the finding (G180).

  • The ClusterNetworkPolicy fix was invisible in the terminal, which is where an operator reads it. G172 moved the verdict to undetermined and the exit code to 1, and put the reason in undeterminedBy and summary -- both JSON keys. _print_reach printed neither, so policy reach showed UNDETERMINED above two lines reading allowed and nothing explaining the contradiction; the policy appeared only inside the trailing not evaluated: line among four other boilerplate caveats. Worse, the read-only check list named adminnetworkpolicy and baselineadminnetworkpolicy but not clusternetworkpolicy, so an operator following the printed advice could not resolve the verdict it had just been given. The terminal now prints unknown because: <policy> is not evaluated here and the summary sentence above the checks, and get clusternetworkpolicy -o yaml goes first in the list when that is what made the answer unknown. A run with no such policy is byte-identical. This is the day's own lesson one layer up: a disclosure is not a decision, and fixing it in the JSON did not fix it in the surface a person reads (G177).

  • The first version of that test passed against the wrong text. It partitioned the output on the check (read-only) heading, which also swallowed the trailing not evaluated: line -- and that line names the policy, so the assertion went green while the printed commands still could not resolve the verdict. Scoped to the kubectl lines only, it fails before the fix and passes after, which is the difference between a test and a decoration (G178).

  • Stale numbers and an overstated citation in this file, found by a closeout red team. The 4.23 entry said openshift/kubernetes release-4.23 "is taking v1.36.x rebases" -- both rebase PRs, #2801 and #2839, are closed and not merged, so what the evidence shows is a branch and a bot that targets it, not a landed rebase. The commit message that corrected this claimed only the commit message was wrong, while this file carried the same overstatement; it now states the evidence at its real strength, and so does the comment in tests/test_yamlio_catalog.py. The policy_generate cap figures (380,670 and 14,168) were measured before KN-S029 added the 108th generated policy and never said which serialization they used; they are now 392,307 -> 14,334 bytes of compact JSON on the 78-rule set, with the serialization named. docs/roadmap.md called P6 "Not started" for the version ceiling this cycle ships, and described P4's pre-G172 behaviour as current; both corrected. demos/README.md carried three hand-typed video lengths that no longer match the released renders (MCP 70 s and 57 s in two tables, actually 67 s; teaser 30 s, actually 32 s), credited the terminal lines to "the 0.2.2 CLI" when facts.py re-records them against the code being built, and listed the compliance video beside three that ship without saying it is a local render only.

  • The weekly risk review opened an issue a week and never closed the previous one. risk-review.yml searched for an open issue titled with this week and edited it, else created one, and never touched the week before -- so 2026-W44 ("release recommended: no, no action needed this week") sat open from 27 October while W45 replaced it, and by December there would have been six open issues all reporting that nothing is wrong, with any week that did need attention among them. An open issue is a claim that something wants doing; a report that has been superseded is not making that claim. The step now closes every other open risk-review issue with a comment naming the week that replaced it. Two details that matter because this runs unattended: the new issue's number is taken from gh issue create's own output rather than a follow-up search, because GitHub's search index is eventually consistent and an empty result would have closed the issue just written; and the run fails loudly rather than closing anything if the number cannot be established. Both paths were exercised against a stubbed gh before shipping -- creating closes the two superseded issues and not the new one, updating closes only the older one. W44 was closed by hand this once (G176).

  • The KN-N008 deprecation change shipped with no test, so the claim it added was unprotected. The commit added the k8s-kep-5707 citation and rewrote the description to say the field is deprecated in Kubernetes 1.36, and touched no test -- the suite stayed green at exactly the count it had before, which is what a claim with nothing asserting it looks like. Any later edit of that rule could have dropped the citation and the sentence and nothing would have said so. There are now four assertions: the KEP is cited and every citation resolves, the description names KEP-5707 and 1.36 and says "deprecated", the remediation names somewhere to go (KEP-5707 offers no successor inside the Service API), and a Service with externalIPs is still reported while one without it is not -- because a rule whose text improved while its detection broke is worse than leaving the text alone. Verified by mutation: removing the citation from rules.py fails test_the_rule_cites_the_kep_that_removes_the_field, so the assertion is real and not incidentally true (G175).

  • KN-N008 knew Service.spec.externalIPs was dangerous but not that it is now going away. The rule already carried CVE-2020-8554 and the DenyServiceExternalIPs admission controller; it predates KEP-5707, which deprecates the field in Kubernetes 1.36 behind a kube-proxy feature gate AllowServiceExternalIPs, plans to switch that gate off by default about four releases later and to lock it off three releases after that, and offers no replacement inside the Service API -- pointing instead to a LoadBalancer Service with a cloud provider, an Ingress controller, or the Gateway API, which it recommends for new deployments. The rule now says so, which turns "this is risky" into "this is risky and it is leaving on a schedule" and changes how a team prioritises it.

Two things are deliberately not asserted. The tentative release numbers (1.40 and 1.43) are given as tentative, because the KEP marks them so. And the gate's default at 1.36 is left out entirely: the KEP contradicts itself about it, with the Summary saying default: false and Phase 1 saying default: true, so there is no version of that sentence this rule can state as fact.

No new rule, no count change: the check, its fixture, its CEL and its controls were already right. Only the citation list (now including KEP-5707) and the explanation moved.

  • New rule KN-S029: a host on a probe or lifecycle handler, and the Pod Security denial it causes. A host on an httpGet or tcpSocket handler makes the kubelet -- a trusted node component -- open a connection to that address on the pod's behalf, so anyone who can create a pod can have the kubelet reach somewhere the pod itself may not be allowed to, including node-local endpoints no NetworkPolicy sees. KEP-4940 adds this to the Pod Security baseline as the probeHost check, enabled by a namespace's pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce-version: v1.34 or later.

Both sides of the deploy were blind to it. audit did not look at the field, so nothing caught it before admission; KN-S029 now checks all ten paths the KEP names -- the three probes' httpGet.host and tcpSocket.host and the postStart/preStop handlers -- for containers and initContainers alike. And policy explain had no entry for the check, so a real denial fell through to the generic branch, which echoes the denial back as its own "fix" and attributes no rule, while readOnlyNextSteps still offered kube-noob fix, which could do nothing for it. The denial now reports relatedRules: ["KN-S029"] and a fix that names the fields to delete.

The check name was verified against the KEP rather than written from memory: it is probeHost, one word, in a message of the form probeHost (container "liveness" uses probeHost 135.45.63.4). A plausible-looking invention -- "restricted probe hosts" was the first guess -- would have produced a regex matching nothing real, and a test written against the same invention would have passed. A test now pins the name to the KEP's (G174).

  • The one result that could not fit in any caller's context was the one an MCP caller had no way to shrink. policy generate -o FILE stopped inlining the rendered set, but PolicyGenerateIn has no output field and an MCP caller has no filesystem to write to, so that fix reached the audience that was not the problem. Measured on the bundled rule set for OpenShift, policy_generate returned 392,307 bytes of compact JSON, about 98,000 tokens, 94% of it one YAML string -- more than most callers can hold, for a tool that is meant to be called by a model. A result the caller cannot read is worse than one that says how to ask for less. (Every byte count here is compact JSON, separators=(',', ':'), on the 78-rule set as shipped; an earlier draft quoted 380,670, measured before KN-S029 added the 108th generated policy.)

There is now a 128 KiB limit on the inline YAML, chosen from the measurements rather than picked: every rule on every engine is 392,307 bytes and every rule on one engine is still 186,000-201,000, while ten rules on two engines is 79,217 and five on one engine is 19,670. The limit sits above the queries a caller can use and below the ones that overflow it. Over the limit the result is the counts, skipped, yamlOmitted: true, the byte count (yamlBytes: 369793) and a sentence naming the three ways out -- rules, a single engines entry, or the CLI's -o. That turns 392,307 bytes into 14,334 (about 98,000 tokens into 3,600, a 96.3% reduction) with nothing a caller reasoned over removed. A request already under the limit is byte-identical to before, and allow_large: true returns the whole set for a caller that knows its own budget.

It refuses rather than truncates, deliberately: half a policy set is not a policy set, and a caller that applied one would enforce some rules and silently drop the rest -- a quiet partial answer, which this project treats as a wrong one (G173).

  • The removed-API tables say where they stop, so "no removals" stops meaning "nobody looked". REMOVED_APIS was read from the Kubernetes deprecated-API migration guide at 1.37 and the guide lists no removals after v1.32, so for any target up to 1.37 an empty answer is complete. Past it, api_history returned removedInTarget: [] and validate produced no removed-API finding, and neither said that nobody had checked that minor -- a verdict earned by absence, the same shape as G163. It stops being hypothetical on 16 December 2026, when Kubernetes 1.38 is released, and already today for --version 4.25, which maps to 1.38. catalog.CHECKED_THROUGH now names the ceiling; api_history returns checkedThrough and beyondCheckedVersions and says so in its note; and a report whose target is past the ceiling carries a notCheckedBeyond block, printed above the findings in text so a short clean-looking report cannot be read as clean. A target at or below the ceiling is byte-identical to what it was, so nothing that was already complete changed shape (G171).

  • policy reach answered "allowed" on a cluster whose real policy it cannot read, and exited 0. ClusterNetworkPolicy (policy.networking.k8s.io/v1alpha2) is cluster-scoped and decided before any NetworkPolicy, and this evaluator does not read it. The kind was already named under notEvaluated and already attached a caveat to every proposed manifest -- but the verdict still read allowed and the CLI still returned 0, so a CI gate merged on an answer a Deny in that policy reverses. A note in a JSON list does not stop a pipeline. With such a policy in the input the verdict is now undetermined, undeterminedBy names it, the summary says which way the NetworkPolicies alone would have gone and that the unread policy can reverse it either way, and the exit code is 1. Deliberately not denied: nothing here knows what the unread policy says, so unknown is the honest answer -- the same third answer a same-priority AdminNetworkPolicy tie already gets. A run with no such policy is unchanged, verdict and exit code both.

This is not a future-API edge case. Calico 3.32 (13 May 2026) removed AdminNetworkPolicy and BaselineAdminNetworkPolicy enforcement outright in favour of ClusterNetworkPolicy, so on a current Calico cluster the policy that actually decides the traffic is the one kind this evaluator cannot read (G172).

  • The debug/anp.py docstring said v1alpha2 kept the same action semantics. It does not. Six differences are now written out where anyone extending the module will read them, each one a way the existing evaluator would misread a v1alpha2 document: Allow is renamed Accept, so ANP_ACTIONS rejects every valid Admin rule; Pass is legal in the Baseline tier, which BANP_ACTIONS forbids; ports is replaced by protocols, and because _port_matches reads an absent ports key as "every port", adding the kind to collect without rewriting the matcher would read a rule scoped to one port as a blanket rule and widen every verdict -- worse than not reading the kind at all; the Baseline tier is no longer a singleton named default; egress peers gain nodes, networks and domainNames, none evaluated; and the per-list cap fell from 100 to 25.

  • OpenShift 4.23 is written out as Kubernetes 1.36, and the evidence for it is stated at its real strength. OCP_TO_K8S stopped at 4.22 and answered 4.23 through the "OCP 4.x tracks 1.(x+13)" extrapolation. The row is now explicit, and the test moved to 4.24 so the extrapolation branch stays covered instead of being shadowed. What the evidence shows: openshift/kubernetes has a release-4.23 branch and its automated rebase bot targets v1.36.x at it -- but both rebase PRs, #2801 (v1.36.5, 28 September 2026) and #2839 (v1.36.6, 2 November 2026), are closed, not merged. That is the direction of travel, not a landed rebase, and not a settled mapping. An earlier draft of this entry and of the commit message said the branch "is taking" those rebases, which overstates it; the row is behaviourally a no-op either way, since the +13 cadence answers 1.36 on its own.

  • The FedRAMP 20x KSI mapping now has a release and a retirement list recorded before it is built. FRMR.KSI.key-security-indicators.json is at release 25.11C (published 2025-12-01), 20x is at current_status: Phase 2 Pilot, the eleven families are intact, and seven indicators carry retired: true: KSI-CMT-05, KSI-MLA-03, KSI-MLA-04, KSI-MLA-06, KSI-SVC-03, KSI-TPR-01, KSI-TPR-02. Evidence offered against a withdrawn indicator is worse than no evidence, so docs/roadmap.md N3 and docs/compliance.md now say that the allowlist ships with the release string and excludes those seven. Documentation only; no code claims a KSI yet.

  • Compaction skipped the excepted findings, so it did nothing for the callers who need it most. compact_report hoisted the per-rule constants out of findings, but Toolkit.audit puts the findings an exceptions file accepted in a separate top-level excepted list, and that list was left untouched -- so every caller with an exceptions file, which is every CI caller, got a report with hoisted findings beside un-hoisted excepted ones, each still carrying its rule's title, severity, stage, category, remediation, citations, stackroxPolicies and controls inline at about 794 bytes apiece. expand_report round-tripped findings alone, so expanding such a report produced two finding lists of different shapes. Both lists are now hoisted against one shared ruleInfo map and both are restored by expand_report; the per-finding exception block is never hoisted. With one exception on examples/netpol-shop.yaml the result goes 39 025 -> 24 674 bytes and the round trip through Toolkit.audit is exact. The existing round-trip test could not have caught this: it called analyze(...).as_dict() directly and so never produced an excepted key at all (G169).

  • policy generate -o FILE reported a byte count that was wrong on Windows. The count was len(text.encode("utf-8")) while the file was written with write_text, which translates \n to \r\n on Windows -- so the file was larger than the number returned, and a caller checking the size would have seen a mismatch on one platform only. It now writes the encoded bytes directly (G170).

  • Filing an exception deleted the control it was filed against from the compliance record. Report.as_dict builds controlSummary from self.findings, and the exceptions filter in Toolkit.audit replaced that list with the surviving findings before calling it -- so a control whose only evidence was an excepted finding dropped out of the report entirely. On examples/netpol-shop.yaml, one well-formed exception against KN-N001 removed AC-4, SC-7 and SC-7(5) from controlSummary, leaving them indistinguishable from controls no rule had ever evaluated. That is backwards: an exception is an owned, reviewed, expiring decision about a control, and the one thing a compliance record must never do is make a signed-off control look unexamined. controls.summary now takes the excepted rule ids as well and counts them apart, so those controls stay named with findings: 0, excepted: 1 and the note says what that means. A run with no exceptions is byte-for-byte what it was (G168).

  • Two results were mostly copies of themselves, and an MCP result is spent out of the caller's context window. audit_manifests carried each rule's title, severity, stage, category, remediation, citations, stackroxPolicies and controls on every finding that rule produced: on examples/ that is 105 findings over 19 distinct rules, so each constant appeared about five times and 53.9% of the 118 631-byte result was duplicates (~29 700 tokens). audit --compact, and compact=true on the tool, now hoist those fields once into a ruleInfo map keyed by rule id -- 118 631 -> 54 694 bytes, 37.9% on a single file -- and audit.compact.expand_report is an exact inverse, asserted by a round-trip test, so this is a hoist and not a guess about which fields a caller does not need. It is opt-in: the flat shape is what /v1/audit callers parse, so the default does not move under them. The exit code is computed from the audit, never from the emitted copy, because compaction moves severity off the findings and a gate must read the same verdict either way (G165, G166).

  • policy generate -o FILE wrote the file and still inlined all 358 KB of YAML, so asking for a file made the result bigger. 385 965 bytes with -o against 385 866 without, about 96 500 tokens either way, for a caller that had just said where it wanted the bytes. With -o the result is now the path, the byte count and the structured summary: 19 059 bytes, ~4 800 tokens, a 95% reduction. Without -o the YAML is still returned inline, because there is nowhere else to put it (G167).

  • Only four of the nineteen readers of untrusted JSON counted its depth first, and from Python 3.14 nothing else does. sanitize.json_nesting_ok was written because json.loads recurses, and it was wired into the four paste paths (server/rest, insight, signals/alertmanager, evals/problems) and into none of the fifteen parsers that read a scanner report, an SBOM, a ticket export, an OCI image config or a corpus shard. That was the same defect as G146 -- a guard added on one input path and not the rest -- and 3.14 made it reachable: sys.getrecursionlimit() no longer bounds json.loads, so on 3.14.6 at the default limit of 1000 a document 10 000 brackets deep parses, and whether 100 000 fails depends on the C stack the process happens to have. The same file is therefore refused in a small container and accepted on a CI runner, which is exactly what ci/test (3.14) was reporting. There is no depth a handler can be written against, so every reader now goes through sanitize.loads_bounded, which counts brackets before parsing and raises sanitize.JsonTooDeep (a ValueError, so existing handlers keep catching it) at the same depth on every interpreter and every machine. Files get a higher ceiling than pastes (512 against 64): nested CycloneDX components are a dependency tree and vuln.reports is built to accept a deep one and bound its own walk at MAX_DEPTH, so refusing at 64 would have turned truncation into rejection. The count itself is two passes in C rather than a Python loop over every character, which is about 0.4 s on a 50 MB document instead of 3 s, and allocates less than the parse it guards. Three tests that asserted whichever message the interpreter happened to produce now assert the refusal.

  • A red team of that change found that it was shallower than it claimed, in five ways. The sweep behind it matched doc|raw_doc|payload = json.loads(, so it reached 18 sites out of 71 and the regression test it left behind could see exactly one of them while its name promised all of them -- G147 again, a test policing a rule being itself a claim. Four of the fifteen "file" parsers were not file reads at all but parse(data) helpers fed by MCP tool arguments on the shared HTTP server, so a pasted 503-deep SARIF was accepted through code_report; max_depth now defaults to the tight ceiling, because a string argument is a paste until proven otherwise, and the callers that read a path ask for the loose one. Four paste paths had no count at all and let a RecursionError out of a read_only tool: rhacs.load (rhacs_compare), the exception list in audit.exceptions.review (audit_manifests, which the browser playground offers), and the JSON array lifted out of a pasted Containerfile in build.containerfile and code.ship. YAML was worse than JSON and unguarded: yaml.CSafeLoader segfaults on a deep document on 3.13 already, no 3.14 needed, so sanitize now carries yaml_nesting_ok, safe_load_bounded and safe_load_all_bounded, counting depth from yaml.parse's event stream, which recurses in neither implementation. gate read operator YAML with except yaml.YAMLError only -- which does not catch RecursionError, since yaml.YAMLError is not a ValueError -- and a deep file ended the command instead of being reported against its path. The regression test is now a frozen inventory of the 67 raw loaders left in the tree that may shrink and never grow, and it says so rather than claiming they are all safe.

  • policy reach decided an undefined case by the order the manifests arrived in. Two AdminNetworkPolicy objects at the same priority whose rules disagree have no defined winner -- the API says so, and OVN-Kubernetes raises ANPWithDuplicatePriority -- but the evaluator sorted on (priority, document index) and returned the first one's action. The same two manifests therefore gave denied in one file order and allowed in the other, with no hint in either answer that anything was in doubt, while the README promises both policy commands "say what they could not determine". The admin tier is now evaluated in priority groups: a group whose matching policies disagree returns a third verdict, undetermined, which names both policies, prints as UNKNOWN in the text output, exits non-zero, and proposes no NetworkPolicy -- because a NetworkPolicy cannot override the admin tier whichever way the tie lands. Two policies tied at one priority that agree still decide, and a duplicate priority is reported as a note either way, since the configuration is relying on undefined behaviour even in the runs where it happens to work.

  • The drift report told you a critical finding had been fixed when all that had happened was losing permission to look. drift.collect_live returns what it could not read, and snapshot, diff and markdown never received it: the CLI printed the first five entries to stderr, the scheduled job logged the first three, and nothing durable held any of it. So when a service account lost list networkpolicies between two runs, the objects were simply absent from the second snapshot, and the daily report said they were removed and their findings fixed -- an RBAC regression published as a security improvement, in the one artefact an operator reads every morning, and served verbatim over MCP by posture_report. attack_paths already carries its notRead into its output and build audit does the same, so drift was the exception rather than an oversight nobody had had the idea for. The snapshot now keeps the record and the kinds behind it; diff holds back any object or finding whose kind the new snapshot was refused, under undetermined rather than removed and fixed; and the Markdown says so in the summary line, above every count, because each of those counts is only over what could be read. A genuinely deleted object is still a removal, and an unreadable resource does not launder an unrelated one. posture_report also gained ageSeconds, which the RHACS output beside it already had: the daily job swallows its own failures so one bad run cannot stop the service, which leaves the previous report as the latest one, and without an age a month-old report read exactly like this morning's. The test that should have caught all of this asserted that collect_live produced the record and then built a snapshot on the next line without it, covering the collecting half only.

  • A refresh that found nothing destroyed the corpus, marked it redistributable, and published an empty database image under a green check. Four defects composed. corpus.write_shard calls os.replace unconditionally, so a source that yielded no records truncated the shard it was replacing to 0 bytes and the records were gone from the data directory. redistributable was all(r["redistributable"] for r in records), and all([]) is True, so the empty shard was marked publishable. corpus build returned 0 if all(isinstance(v, int) ...), and a count of 0 is an int, so only an exception gave a non-zero exit. And db_bundle.build refused only when there were no shards at all, so a present-but-empty shard shipped -- _shard_problems finds no bad lines in a file with no lines, so db-check reported it clean. Upstream moving a documentation path is enough to set it off: ingest matches nothing and raises nothing. write_shard now refuses a zero-record write unless the caller passes allow_empty, keeping the previous shard; an empty shard is not redistributable; the bundle refuses a corpus with no records; and the two workflows gained the records gate the Jira step beside them already had.

  • Nine workflow steps lost the exit code of the command they ran. GitHub's default shell is bash -e without pipefail, so kube-noob corpus build ... | tee summary.json reports tee's status, not the build's. The two corpus workflows run eight such pipelines between them, including the one that packs the published database bundle, so a command could fail outright and the step still pass. Each now sets set -euo pipefail.

  • Five more places reported a clean result without having checked anything. gate's notRun listed only the two absent-report cases, so a pipeline whose manifest glob stopped matching -- a renamed deploy/ directory -- kept passing its scanner and SARIF reports, exited 0, printed "gate passed", and rendered an empty "Not run" section, which positively asserts that nothing was left out; it now names the no-manifests and no-Containerfile cases. An eval assertion whose operator was misspelled (equal for equals) scored as a pass: _check_value returns None for an unrecognised operator and _assert turned that into "present" -- on a gating, weight-2.0 assertion, which also disengaged the GATE_CAP clamp, so a valid-but-wrong answer scored high instead of being capped at 0.4. validate_assertion now refuses a spec that names no operator, and evaluate calls it. The Jira scrubber's account-id pattern was strictly weaker than the privacy audit that gates the weekly refresh -- the audit matches bare ids, the scrubber required a [~...] or accountId: prefix -- so a reporter id pasted bare into a description survived the scrub and then failed the gate: not a leak, because no workflow commits Jira data and the gate exits 1, but one such ticket failed the refresh every week and discarded an hour of paced fetching each time. Both now share one definition. db_bundle's MAX_MEMBER and MAX_MEMBERS were referenced only inside the reader, so build() could pack an archive nothing can import and report success, and check() on a directory enforced neither; check now applies the predicate and build runs check over its staging tree, so there is one call site rather than two that can drift. And three assertions ended in or True, including test_a_blind_run_is_not_reported_as_a_clean_one, whose comment states the repo's own coverage-honesty property while the assertion that would hold it was dead. All three pass without it.
  • Three scorers reported success for a spec that asserted nothing. The same shape as the corpus redistributable bug: all([]) is True and an empty collection is the flattering answer. evals/reward.py set coverage = 1.0 if not must else len(hits) / len(must), so a qa task listing no mustMention gave any prose at all a perfect coverage score. Evaluation.passed was self.parsed and all(o.passed for o in self.outcomes), so an evaluation with zero outcomes reported passed: true while score returned 0.0 -- two fields of one result disagreeing. And selftest's audit case built missing = [r for r in case["expect"] if r not in fired] and returned not missing, so a case with expect: [] passed whatever the audit found, in the command a user runs to decide whether to trust the tool. None was reachable from a shipped fixture, which is exactly why none was caught: the version of this bug that was reachable published an empty corpus. Each now refuses an empty spec, and a sweep asserts that no shipped fixture asserts nothing, so the next empty one is caught when it is added rather than when it scores.

A red-team pass of the same family found two more, one of them worse than the three above. corpus db-check reported clean on a bundle manifest declaring no shards -- the guard against an empty corpus had gone into build(), which stops kube-noob from publishing one, but db-check is the command a user runs against a bundle they downloaded, and it printed ok and exited 0 on an empty one. That was the last open link of that chain, at the end people actually hold. And run_protocol reported status: "ready" when it had audited nothing: "ready" if not blockers else "blocked" asserts readiness from an absence of HIGH findings, and with no documents no rule ran, so there were no blockers -- a far stronger claim than a control summary, from the same root cause as the coverage record still to be built. It now says undetermined.

The sweep is a frozen inventory rather than a lint rule, and the reasoning is worth recording: a pure AST sweep for all(...), not any(...) and X if not Y else Z finds 85 sites, and the live bugs shared no AST shape -- two ternaries, one all(), one missing guard. A rule precise enough to catch them would fire on all 85 and be silenced within a day. What the real bugs share is a context, not a shape: an empty collection reaching a name that means "this was fine". Filtering on that cuts 85 to 13, which is small enough to review and freeze. - The "rebuilt daily" claim is still live on kube-noob.com, because the test that removed it read one file. G136 exists for this sentence: docs/use-cases.md promised a daily rebuild of the public kube-noob-db image, whose workflow has failed on every scheduled run since it was added and has published nothing. The fix corrected that page, and the enforcing test asserted "rebuilt daily" not in use_cases -- use_cases being docs/use-cases.md and nothing else. The same sentence sat untouched in the chart's values.yaml, which the site republishes at /reference/helm-values/, so the claim stayed on the live site after it was "fixed". That is G147 in its own right: a test that polices a rule is itself a claim, and it is checked by trying to evade it. The chart comment now says the image is not published and gives the two commands that do exist, and the test reads every tracked text file, with three files allowlisted by name because they quote the phrase in order to record that it was wrong. Proven by reverting the chart alone: the widened test fails, the old one could not see the file.

  • The roadmap was a year-old shape with this week's findings missing from it. It still said "updated after 0.5.0" while 0.6.0, 0.7.0 and 0.7.1 had shipped, listed the OpenShift Helm catalog and the plugin directory as "not started" and "next" when one has never merged an OWNERS pull request and the other has ten versions held by a policy its own checklist says is held by design, and carried no entry for the two scheduled workflows that have been failing for days. It is rewritten around one ordering -- correctness before capability, capability before reach, reach before breadth -- with every item dated to a release, a What this refuses to be section naming the privileges and claims the tool will not take and why, an honest ceiling on the compliance story (27 of about 1,189 controls, evidence rather than coverage, never a percentage), and a state column on the riskiest assumptions that says which are blocked on a design partner rather than on code.

  • A red team of this release's own changes found eight defects in them, including one that undid the change's own purpose. doctor still printed posture: OK on its first line with CONFLICT: on its last, because posture() computes status before the model conflicts are appended -- the exact signal the change set out to correct, and the test could not see it because it asserted only the conflict list and the exit code. The same check then refused a supported configuration: openai-compatible with no apiKeyEnv is the documented no-key path (agent/providers.py: "the request carries no Authorization header at all"), and the only provider the offline lock allows; it is a conflict only for a provider that has a default key variable.

  • The drift alarm was switched off rather than fixed. Treating any non-release ref as a notice meant that while kube-noob.com served main -- which it does, and which is the only way to publish a correction before the next tag -- a release whose deploy never ran would never be reported. The branch case now compares the version the build recorded and falls through to the age check when the site is behind.
  • policy reach was honest about a ClusterNetworkPolicy only for pasted files. inventory_from_cluster read the two v1alpha1 tiers and not clusternetworkpolicies, so against a live cluster -- the answer an operator trusts more, because it came from their cluster -- the kind was still dropped in silence with no caveat.   • corpus-refresh failed on a missing passphrase only after an hour of paced fetching, and told the reader to "run with fresh=true", which does not gate that step. The check now runs first, and says so.

  • Three tests were weaker than they read. The missing-secret scanner matched one exact shape, so 13 of 14 ways of writing the same guard slipped past -- including "$X_TOKEN" without braces, a form already in jira-db-image.yml; it now reads the guard's own line, stops at the matching fi, and treats any non-zero exit as honest. The address scan covered 452 of 968 tracked files, missing pyproject.toml (which ships to PyPI) and the plugin manifest (which ships to the directory); it now reads every tracked text file, and its allowlist no longer admits example.io, example-corp.com or a noreply@ local part at any domain. The plugin README said "Every one of the 32 is pinned to an exact version and SHA-256 hash" when one of the 32 is the plugin's own virtual project with no artifact to hash.

  • The plugin README denied dependencies its own lock file ships, on the live site and in a review queue. It read "There is no HTTP client and no web server among them -- so the installed tree cannot reach the network even if something asked it to", and listed eight dependencies "and no others". plugin/kube-noob/uv.lock names 32 packages, among them httpx2, httpcore2, h11, uvicorn and sse-starlette: mcp declares its HTTP transports as dependencies whether or not a plugin uses them. A reviewer can open the lock file, so the claim was both false and trivially falsifiable. An earlier pass removed a neighbouring false sentence and left this one. The README now states the real count and names what arrives through mcp, and makes the narrower claim that is actually checkable: the plugin serves stdio only, and the bundled modules under server/ never import those packages, start a server or open a connection. Three tests read the README against uv.lock in both directions, so prose and lock file cannot drift apart again.

  • Three more jobs reported success while skipping the work they exist to do. The site job of a release, the notify job of site-sync and the corpus encrypt step each warned about a missing secret and exit 0, so a release could finish green having never told the website about itself -- which is how kube-noob.com served the previous release's videos under a new release's banner for a day with no red check anywhere. They now fail and say which secret to add. The one guard that genuinely continues another way keeps exit 0 and must say so, in a # honest-skip: note the test reads. And site-sync's drift check compared versions with string equality, so a site serving a ref newer than the latest release was reported as a failed deploy (it went red on 2026-11-01 for exactly that reason, while nothing was wrong), and a site deliberately built from a branch -- the only way to publish a correction before the next tag -- was reported as broken. It now compares with sort -V and treats a non-release ref as a notice.

  • A real email address and a private backlog were published in the public repository. docs/openshift-release.md told the reader to run git config --global user.email and filled the command in with the maintainer's own address: an Apache-2.0 repository is a scraping target, and an address in a document cannot be withdrawn once a fork or an archive has it. The command now names placeholders the reader fills in, and says why. .claude/loop/ -- the build queue the build-loop skill reads, an unreleased backlog with the product's own moat notes in it -- had three files tracked, so anyone reading the repository read the roadmap; it stays on disk and is now ignored. Two tests enforce both: no tracked file may carry an address outside the RFC 2606 reserved names, and nothing under .claude/loop/ may be tracked. Git's own commit metadata is left alone, because rewriting published history is worse than the exposure.

  • A ClusterNetworkPolicy in the input was dropped in silence, and the fix that followed was wrong. AdminNetworkPolicy and BaselineAdminNetworkPolicy (policy.networking.k8s.io/v1alpha1) are listed by kubernetes-sigs/network-policy-api as "no longer actively developed"; the API under active development is ClusterNetworkPolicy v1alpha2, one cluster-scoped CRD carrying both tiers in spec.tier, enforced today by Calico 3.32, Cilium 1.20, Kube-OVN and kube-network-policies. kube-noob did not know the kind, so an Admin-tier Deny written in the current API never appeared in the answer: policy reach attributed the denial to the NetworkPolicy tier and proposed a NetworkPolicy that the admin tier overrides -- applied, changing nothing, which is the one outcome netpol.py already refuses to produce for the v1alpha1 tier. Until the evaluator reads v1alpha2, the kind is named under "not evaluated" and every namespace-scoped fix carries a caveat saying a cluster-scoped policy decided first and how to check it. The caveat prints in the text output, not only the JSON.

  • The chart's natural OpenShift exposure was silently broken. route.enabled: true with the default empty route.host lets OpenShift generate <name>-<namespace>.<apps-domain> -- a hostname the chart cannot predict and therefore never adds to server.allowedHosts -- so every MCP call came back 421 Invalid Host header while the liveness probe, the readiness probe and helm test all stayed green, because they reach the Service, not the Route. Ingress never had this: its template marks the host required. The chart now refuses to render and says how to find the domain. No test covered any of the chart's fail guards, which is how this shipped. The guard immediately turned CI red: our own Audit our own chart (OpenShift ...) step had been rendering route.enabled=true with no host since the Route was added, so the project had been auditing an exposure that would 421. The step now sets a host, and a test refuses any workflow that turns the Route on without one in the same step. The chart README and values.yaml now say the host is required and why.

  • The README's second runnable command could not be run. It read kube-noob policy explain -f denial.txt and no such file ships, so the first thing a stranger copied answered error: no such file: denial.txt. It now points at examples/denial-multi.txt, a single paste in which Pod Security, Kyverno, Gatekeeper, ValidatingAdmissionPolicy and OpenShift SCC all rejected the same Deployment, each in its own wording -- the shortest demonstration of the one-pass attribution the README leads with. -f k8s/ became -f your-manifests/, since that one is the reader's to supply, and a test now refuses any README -f path that neither ships nor reads as the reader's own. The README also claimed both commands "exit non-zero for CI"; only reach does, and that is right for an explainer, so the claim was corrected rather than the code.

  • kube-noob doctor called a model path healthy that could not run. With a provider configured, no SDK installed and no key set it printed posture: OK, while its own help promises "exit 1 on conflicts". Both reasons are knowable without calling anything, and it now names them and exits 1. provider: none stays quiet.

  • Nothing linked kube-noob.com. Not project.urls, not the README, not the plugin manifest, not the chart: a stranger arriving from PyPI or GitHub could not find the site where the demos, the recordings and the browsable rules are. PyPI's Homepage now points there and the README names it.

  • The README buried the two things no other tool does. It opened with a five-bullet feature list; the differentiators -- which tier of AdminNetworkPolicy/NetworkPolicy/BaselineAdminNetworkPolicy governs a connection, and which of six admission systems denied a pod -- were a clause in the third bullet. Both now lead, with a runnable command each against an example that ships, and the feature list is one paragraph pointing at docs/use-cases.md. Two independent reviews this week failed to discover kube-noob gate at all, which is the same problem seen from outside.

  • A documented feature had never been published. docs/use-cases.md said the public kube-noob-db image is "rebuilt daily" and gave cosign and helm commands for pulling it. The db-image workflow has failed on every scheduled run since it was added and has published nothing. The page now says so, and gives the two commands that do exist for building one yourself.

  • The audit scored a file it had not audited. Pasted a Helm values.yaml, it read the whole document as one unnamed Kubernetes object, reported that apiVersion was missing, and returned 90 out of 100 -- for a file whose images were :latest and whose securityContext and resources were empty. A score says the rules were applied, so a score on a file the rules never saw is a wrong answer, not a thin one. audit, validate and redteam now exit 2 when no document carries both apiVersion and kind, naming what the file looks like and the command that would make it auditable. One Kubernetes document anywhere in the input is enough to proceed.

  • kube-noob's knowledge had nothing about kube-noob. Asked for its own CI story the search returned Kyverno and RHACS documents, so a client wrote kube-noob audit -f rendered.yaml # check the CLI's --help for the flag and never mentioned gate, the command built for exactly that job. kube-noob-cli.md now covers gate and its SARIF, summary and code-quality outputs, the exit codes, the ExceptionList schema, what the audit reads, and the command table. Tests hold its exit codes to the CLI and run its exception example through the real loader.

  • docs/operations.md says what to do when a release job fails after the other four have published, because the obvious move is the wrong one: deleting the release destroys the assets the run already built, and re-pushing the tag re-runs publish-pypi, which cannot succeed on an immutable version, so github-release never runs at all. Re-running the single job takes the retained artifacts and re-runs videos and site behind it; videos.yml has a workflow_dispatch for re-attaching the videos to a release that already exists.

  • The plugin declared two dependencies it never imports and imported three it never declared. httpx and uvicorn belong to the web client and the HTTP service, neither of which ships in the plugin, while anyio, mcp-types and pydantic-core are imported directly by the bundled server and were reaching it only through another package's requirements -- an upgrade that dropped one would have broken the plugin with nothing to say so. The manifest now matches the imports in both directions, with a test each way, and the resolved tree lost httpx, httpcore and certifi: 35 packages down to 32, with no HTTP client among them.

  • The plugin README said the launcher would switch to a published package from 0.7.1. It did not, and should not: a launcher that names a package and a version fixes that package and leaves the rest of the tree to resolve on the user's machine, where this one resolves nothing. The README now says what the plugin does and why, and a test holds it there rather than enforcing the abandoned plan, as it had been doing.

  • A release the maintainer created by hand took the whole publish pipeline down with it. github-release ran gh release create, which is an error when the release already exists, so on v0.7.1 the job failed and videos and site were skipped behind it: the release carried no wheel, no chart, no skill ZIP and no videos, and kube-noob.com was never told the version had changed -- while PyPI, the chart repository, the MCP registry and Homebrew had all published successfully. Creating the release first is an ordinary thing to do, so the step now branches on whether it exists and uploads with --clobber, which also makes re-running the job safe.

  • The registry-allowlist chart guard never passed once since G243 wrote it (G261). ci/helm-and-dogfood was red on the step that proves audit.allowedRegistries reaches both config files -- the sidecar's and the admission webhook's -- which is what makes kube-noob audit and the webhook give one verdict. The cause was shell, not chart: reg="--set audit.allowedRegistries={...}" used as "$reg" is one argv element, so helm answered unknown flag: --set audit.allowedRegistries, the pipe fed nothing to python, and yaml.safe_load returned None. Its neighbour $base is unquoted and splits correctly.

reg is a bash array now, expanded "${reg[@]}". Because that mistake is invisible on review, tests/test_workflow_honesty.py scans every workflow for a flag bundle passed as a single argument. It joins backslash continuations first, so a quoted element of a for args in "$a" "$b" list -- correct, since cmd $args splits it later -- is not flagged; written before the fix, it named both real sites and neither false positive. A guard that cannot pass is not a guard, and the property it was meant to hold was never checked in CI until now.

  • ci/test (3.13) was red because of a missing numpy, not a Python version (G260). Three of the four matrix jobs were green and one was not, which reads as a 3.13 incompatibility and is nothing of the kind. tests/test_video_loudness.py guards its module on ffmpeg and ffprobe; one test inside it also did a bare import audio_kit, which needs numpy -- and numpy is in no dev extra, because the videos job installs it with ffmpeg and runs this file itself. A runner without ffmpeg skipped the module and never reached the import; the one runner that had ffmpeg ran it and raised ModuleNotFoundError. The verdict came from which binaries the runner happened to carry.

It is pytest.importorskip("audio_kit") now -- the idiom tests/test_video_voice.py already used for the same module -- so the test runs wherever numpy is present (it still does, in videos and locally) and says so where it is not. Reproduced on a clean 3.12 environment exactly as on 3.13, which is what ruled the version out.

  • ksi --html wrote no file at all, and the crosswalk's masthead counted indicators while calling them findings (G259). U5 rebuilt the top of the page from each report's own keys, and every one of its unit tests built its report by hand -- so nobody ran the command. An indicator row's findings is an integer count where a posture report's is a bucket dict, the masthead read it as a dict, and AttributeError came out of the one artifact that leaves the terminal. The reader is type-guarded now (a shape it does not recognise produces a zero it can defend, never an exception), and every --html path -- gate, ksi, posture diff, posture report -- is rendered end to end through the CLI in a test, because a hand-built fixture is a guess about the shape and the shape was the defect.

The wording defect underneath it was G257's own rule, broken in the page that introduced G257: one sentence served all three report shapes, so the crosswalk announced "21 findings are open, and 15 rules reached no verdict" above tiles reading "indicators", and sent the reader to a section 1 the crosswalk does not have. The verdict and the tiles are now built in the same branch, in that shape's own units, naming only a section that shape renders -- the crosswalk says how many indicators have findings against them and how many have no kube-noob rule behind them at all, which is the number the page exists to report.

  • ClusterNetworkPolicy v1alpha2 is evaluated, not just named (P4, G258). The current policy.networking.k8s.io API replaces AdminNetworkPolicy and BaselineAdminNetworkPolicy with one cluster-scoped kind carrying both tiers, and Calico 3.32 removed enforcement of the older pair in favour of it -- so on such a cluster the policy that actually decides was the one kube-noob could only name. policy reach now reads it: the v1alpha2 Admin tier first, then v1alpha1's, then the NetworkPolicies, then v1alpha1's baseline, then the v1alpha2 Baseline tier.

It is a module of its own because four things changed and each would make the older evaluator read a valid policy wrong rather than refuse it: Allow became Accept; ports became protocols (tcp/udp/sctp, each with a destinationPort of a number or a range, plus destinationNamedPort) -- and the old matcher reads an absent ports key as every port, so a rule scoped to one port would have been widened to all of them; the Baseline tier is no longer a singleton named default and carries a priority, so two baseline policies can tie and that tie is now reachable; and every list is capped at 25 rather than 100. Verified against kubernetes-sigs/network-policy-api at 2383f01 rather than from the release notes.

The upstream rule that mattered most is one no earlier note had: a peer carrying no field the reader recognises means a CRD newer than the reader, and the API says to fail closed -- for Accept rules the rule matches no traffic, for Deny and Pass it is a deny-all. Skipping such a peer would let a later rule accept traffic a newer policy denies.

Two consequences had to be fixed with it. Once the kind was evaluated, the caveat that used to warn "a NetworkPolicy cannot override this" went away -- and the answer began proposing exactly that NetworkPolicy against a cluster-scoped Deny, the fix the caveat had existed to prevent. An Admin-tier Deny of either API now routes to the cluster-administrator fix, and says Accept where the API says Accept. And --cni gained its own table for the new kind rather than inverting the old one: "Calico 3.32 does not enforce AdminNetworkPolicy" and "Calico 3.32 enforces ClusterNetworkPolicy" are two sourced facts, and deriving the second from the first would read a CNI that enforces neither as enforcing the new one.

A red-team pass over the finished module found nine more defects before any of it shipped, every one a wrong ALLOW and every one the same shape: making a kind readable moves it onto a different code path, and the guards on the old path do not come with it. The second reading that exists to withhold a tier whose enforcement nobody states was clearing the v1alpha1 tiers only, so both readings always agreed and nothing was ever withheld. A document the reader refused -- a newer apiVersion, a subject shape it does not know, a priority out of range -- became a clean allowed with exit 0, where before the kind was readable ANY such document made the verdict undetermined. destinationNamedPort resolved by number and discarded the protocol, so a rule naming a UDP port accepted TCP on the same number. A Baseline-tier deny took the Admin tier's "no NetworkPolicy can override this" fix, which the spec contradicts outright. Shapes the CRD refuses -- protocols: [], tcp: {}, an empty from, operator: Exist -- were read as "every port" or "matches nothing" instead of as unreadable, and three malformed selectors aborted the whole command with an AttributeError. All fixed, and five tests that had asserted the wrong behaviour now assert the right one.

  • The HTML report reads as a document of record (U5, G257). It is the only thing that leaves the terminal -- a reviewer, an auditor or a manager opens it, and often prints it into an audit package -- and it was about 7 000 pixels of h2 + table with no verdict, no contents and no anchors. Now: a masthead with the verdict as a sentence somebody can paste into a ticket, the three numbers that decide the next action, and a provenance block saying what the numbers are true of; limits of this examination as section 1, numbered and in the document's own voice instead of a strip of yellow boxes a reader learns to skim; sections numbered in the order they are written, with a contents that agrees; an id on every finding and every control row, built from the rule and the resource so it still means the same row tomorrow; controls ranked by consequence with the silent ones folded into a <details> that the print stylesheet forces open; and real print rules -- @page, a repeating thead, no row split across a break. Structure is blue now, so red belongs to findings alone. Still one file: no script, no stylesheet, no font, no image, nothing fetched.

Three defects were caught by looking at the rendered page rather than at the tests. The verdict read "5 findings are open" in the largest type on the page, two sections above a table reading 3: one finding is evidence about several controls, so summing the standing column double-counts it. The gate verdict was printed twice. And the masthead counted the checks nobody ran, then sent the reader to a section 1 that never mentioned them -- a check that did not run is now a limit, which is what it always was.

  • Eighteen typed claims in the documents are now measured from the code (P8, G256). The four defects this item was opened for had already been fixed by generating docs/limits.md's platform table, docs/use-cases.md's compaction sentence and docs/roadmap.md's measured lines -- but generating three sentences left every other number somebody had typed. A guard table now pairs each of those with the thing that measures it, and scripts/doc_numbers.py --check fails when one drifts. It found one live defect immediately: docs/use-cases.md sold the CRD-reach example as "the same 92 rules" while the code had 93. A guard whose sentence has been reworded is an error rather than a skip, because a claim that quietly stops being guarded is how the first four got published. Writing the guard also caught a defect in itself: an indicator cites its controls in lower case and kube-noob names them in upper, so a hand-rolled intersection answered "46 of 46 indicators have no rule behind them" -- a sentence nobody would have questioned. It now asks the crosswalk's own indicator_rules, and says 15.

  • A posture snapshot keeps what the audit had to assume (U4, G255). notRead records what the CLUSTER refused; an assumption records what kube-noob itself could not read -- above all "N pod spec(s) are in kinds kube-noob has no reader for, NOT audited" (G232). The snapshot was throwing those away, so a folder of Argo Rollout snapshots reported a complete read every day on the page a reviewer opens, while a whole class of workload was never looked at. The snapshot keeps them (bounded), the report carries them forward, and posture report --html, its markdown and its text output each state them above the counts they bound -- the HTML page already rendered assumptions as a caveat and had simply never been given any. Found by red-teaming U3 rather than by a user.

  • The HTML report has charts, and they are drawn under rules rather than plotted (U3, G254). gate --html gets findings by severity; posture report --html gets open findings per control, the five standing buckets as a stacked bar per control, and open against excepted findings across the snapshots the folder still keeps. All of it is inline SVG: no script, no stylesheet, no font, no library, nothing the browser fetches, because the constraint that makes the page forwardable to a reviewer on a laptop with no network is the same one that decides how a chart may be drawn. Three rules hold it. A chart shows only what a table on the same page shows, so every bar has a row under it to check -- the severity counts and the per-snapshot counts are now tables that were not there before. Every bar is labelled with its name and its count, and the palettes went through a colour-vision validator in both light and dark rather than being eyeballed, so a reader in print or in forced colours loses nothing. And a count that was withheld is not drawn: the trend is withheld entirely when any snapshot in the series could not read every resource, with the reason and each snapshot's own counts in its place, because a line through a floor publishes a fall in findings that may only be a fall in what kube-noob was allowed to see. A red team of the first version found seven more, all of the same shape and the first of them the sharpest: every report kube-noob wrote before 0.8 carries no record of what a snapshot could not read, under the same format string, so a folder inherited across an upgrade would have drawn a line through up to fourteen points whose counts were floors. A missing record is unknown now, not yes, and unknown withholds the line exactly like a refusal. With it: a kept file that is valid JSON but not a report, or carries a count that is not a number, is named rather than crashing the daily job after it has already written its files; a missing count is no longer coerced to zero; a snapshot whose report was never written is named; a series that mixes a -f run with a --live run is two populations and is not drawn as one line; the chart says how many older points the table has that it does not; four stacked segments at the one-pixel floor no longer push the bar past the plot; an all-zero series no longer labels its axis with a 1 nothing measured; the whole-series byte budget is spent on the newest reports, which is the end a trend is read from; and drift now reads every file it is handed through the depth-guarded loader, which took the last two raw json.loads calls in the module out of the frozen inventory.

The series itself is new -- posture report and the posture_report tool now return a point per retained report -- and it is deliberately not written into the report file, which would nest each report's history inside the next and grow the folder with the square of posture.keep.

  • Four CodeQL alerts on test assertions, fixed rather than suppressed (G253). "docker.io" in message is how URL-validation bugs are written, so the scanner is right to flag the shape wherever it appears -- and it was a weak assertion anyway: it passes on a message that names the registry anywhere, including in a sentence that says the opposite. Each one is a phrase match against the sentence the rule actually produces now, with the dots escaped. Two more of the same shape that the scanner had not reached yet went with them.

  • A crash, two wrong facts about hosted clusters, a false HIGH on KServe's own sample, and nine router defects (G252, from a third red team). The crash first: a bundle with two APIServer objects where one had a non-mapping metadata raised an AttributeError out of the detection block, and kube-noob audit exited 1 with a traceback -- exit 1 means "findings at or above --fail-on", so a projection that crashed reported a verdict it never computed. Then the facts: a hosted control plane's audit policy is generated by HyperShift from its own vendored library-go, which dropped core Events one release later than standalone OpenShift did, so ROSA HCP at 4.18 records no Event activity where standalone 4.18 does; the machineconfiguration.openshift.io group joined the body exclusion only in 4.18, and the manifest spells its key resource rather than resources, which the loader drops -- so from 4.18 it is the whole group rather than two resources; and --version 1.33, a documented input for an OpenShift target, was compared against 4.18 as though 1 were a major, which decided the Events row from a comparison with no meaning. KServe next: the upstream sklearn-iris sample -- model: {modelFormat, storageUri}, no image, because the ServingRuntime supplies it -- was reported as a HIGH "container has no image", and since G248 that denied it at admission. The report now records that the image comes from an operator and was not read. A cost, which is a bypass: the locator scanned every sibling of a list for every finding, so a Deployment padded with 2,000 containers took the webhook six seconds and one with 16,000 took six minutes -- past its three-second timeout, so the API server admitted the object unchecked while the process stayed busy. Those keys are computed once per list now: half a second for 2,000 containers, four and a half for 16,000. And the router: the negative lookaheads G247 added silenced real questions (an exposure question that mentions a repo, a reachability question that names a webhook or a quota), seven of the new routes matched text that was not asking for them, one command could be suggested twice, and one route cost 32 ms on a crafted line. The guard that refuses a word in two lookahead groups now expands rules? to rule and reach(?:es|ed|able)? to all four spellings and refuses a word CONTAINED in another group's phrase, which is what made every rule satisfy both halves of a route written to need two things.

  • fix -f dir/ says that it merged your files, and a finding's line number is a line of a file you can open now (G251). Reading the input pipeline for the line numbers found a second trap in it: fix over a directory reads several files and writes one stream, so a caller redirecting it would have one file where they had five -- it says so on stderr now, where the change summary already goes. kube-noob audit -f dir/ reads one string, because the audit does: the directory's files are concatenated with --- between them, so every line number after the first file was a line of that concatenation -- in a three-file directory, findings in the second file were reported ten or twenty lines past the end of the file they were in, against no path at all. Each finding (and each excepted one) now names its file and carries that file's line, in the text report, the JSON, the compact form and the OSCAL document, which an assessor can now open. A single file is named too; piped input names nothing, because there is nothing to name. The OSCAL document deliberately carries the file and not the line: the line depends on where a document sits inside its file, and that document is held to being identical for a reordered bundle.

  • kubectl debug is checked now: an ephemeral container goes through a subresource nothing was matching (R7, G250). kubectl debug adds its container through pods/ephemeralcontainers, which is a different admission request from the Pod's own creation, so a privileged debug container from an unapproved registry walked past every rule that had just refused the same container in a Deployment. The webhook evaluates that one subresource (every other one -- status, exec, log, portforward -- stays skipped), and two details decide whether it is useful or merely noisy: the owner check does not apply, because no ReplicaSet carries an ephemeral container and skipping it would have made a managed workload's Pod the one request nothing checks; and only what the request ADDS is reported, so a Pod that already breaks an enforced rule can still be debugged. The generated policies are deliberately not extended to the subresource: they have no before-and-after comparison, so one would refuse every debug of an already-failing Pod.

  • The detection-coverage statement was wrong about Events, and five smaller things around it (G249, from a red team of G244/G246). The first version quoted the API type's summary comment, which says core Events are never logged; the rule that dropped them lives in the policy manifests cluster-kube-apiserver-operator generates the real policy from, and it was removed in OpenShift 4.18 -- so on a 4.18+ cluster an Event is recorded, and the block was telling a reader that a deleted Event left no trace in an audit log that has it. Every row is now read from those manifests, with the source named in the output, and the Events row is decided by --version or reported as undecidable without one. Two rows the comment never mentioned are now stated: no profile records the body of a Secret, a ServiceAccount token, a Route, a token review, an OAuthClient or a MachineConfig, and nothing at all is recorded for the discovery and health endpoints. Also fixed: a bundle with two APIServer objects is undecided unless one is the singleton named cluster (it used to read whichever came first), a spec or spec.audit that is not a mapping is undecided rather than reported as the default, the cluster-wide sentence names the groups a customRules entry overrides instead of claiming to cover them, and the block is absent off OpenShift and absent from kube-noob validate, where KN-S035 already declines.

  • Four defects in the KSI projection from the same review (G249).
